Appendix A
Supplementary Information for Section
2

This section collects a number of supplementary remarks relating to Section
2
. Appendix
A.1
briefly outlines Neyman orthogonality; a favourable property our influence functions satisfy. In Appendix
A.2
we derive the form of the semiparametric efficient influence function for the partially parametric model.
Our estimation strategy is interpreted from the perspective of an infinite dimensional extension of the generalised method of moments in Appendix
A.4
. Our robust estimation strategy is also briefly explored within a broader class of models in Appendix
A.5
, where we study as an example instrumental variable models.

A.1
Neyman orthogonality

All influence functions of the partially parametric model as in (
10
) satisfy a so-called Neyman orthogonality
(
Neyman,, 1959
;
Neyman,, 1979
)
property of the following form:

Definition 1

(Neyman Orthogonality)
.

Consider a function
\tilde{\psi}:\mathcal{S}\times\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{\alpha}}\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{\beta}}
where
p_{\alpha},p_{\beta}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the function
\tilde{\psi}
is Neyman orthogonal at the law
P
with respect to the nuisance functions
\rho:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{p_{\alpha}}
and
\zeta:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{p_{\beta}}
if

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{\psi}(S;\theta_{P},\alpha,\zeta(X,Z))|_{\alpha=\rho(Z)}\,|\,Z\big]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\nabla_{\beta}\tilde{\psi}(S;\theta_{P},\rho(Z),\beta)|_{\beta=\zeta(X,Z)}\,|\,X,Z\big]

\displaystyle=0,

almost surely.

Note in particular that in the above we adopt a definition of Neyman orthogonality in terms of Euclidean derivatives as in e.g.
Mackey et al., (2018)
, as opposed to Gâteaux derivatives as in e.g.
Chernozhukov et al., (2018)
. We see functions
\psi
in the class given by (
10
) are indeed Neyman orthogonal at
P
with respect to the nuisance functions
(f_{P},\phi_{P})
for any
\psi_{P}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z]=0
.

A.2
On the semiparametric efficient influence function

Proposition 6
.

The function
\phi_{P}
corresponding to the efficient influence function in the partially parametric model takes the form

(x,z)\mapsto W_{P}(x,z)\bigg(U_{P}(x,z)-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[W_{P}(X,Z)U_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z]}{\mathbb{E}_{P}[W_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z]}\bigg),

where

\displaystyle W_{P}(x,z)=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\varepsilon^{2}(Y,X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]^{-1},

\displaystyle U_{P}(x,z)=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(Y,X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X=x,Z=z],

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x,y,z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(z)):=\frac{y-\mu(x,z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(z))}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\big|_{\gamma=f_{P}(z)}\mu(x,z;\theta_{P},\gamma)},

with
W_{P}
given up to a constant of proportionality.

Proof.

Recall the notation
S:=(Y,X,Z)
. Then the asymptotic variance of the estimator derived from the (scaled) influence function
\psi
as in (
10
) is

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z),\phi_{P}(X,Z))\right]}{\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\psi(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z),\phi_{P}(X,Z))\right]\right)^{2}},

where

\displaystyle\psi(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z),\phi_{P}(X,Z))=\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\phi_{P}(X,Z)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z)):=\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\Big|_{\gamma=f(Z)}\mu(X,Z;\theta,\gamma)\Big)^{-1}\big(Y-\mu(X,Z;\theta,f(Z))\big),

and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z]=0
.
Then

\displaystyle\quad\;\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z),\phi_{P}(X,Z))\right]}{\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\psi(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z),\phi_{P}(X,Z))\right]\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}^{2}(X,Z)\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))]}{(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))])^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}^{2}(X,Z)\mathbb{E}_{P}[\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z]]}{(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z]])^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}^{2}(X,Z)\mathbb{E}_{P}[\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z]]}{(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z]-\varphi_{P}(Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\}])^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\right]-\varphi_{P}(Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\big)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\right]}\right]^{-1},

where

\varphi_{P}(Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\\
:=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\right]}\,\Big|\,Z\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\right]}\,\Big|\,Z\right],

and recalling that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z]=0
.
Note the final inequality follows as a result of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Equality in the above holds when

\displaystyle\phi_{P}(X,Z)=W_{P}(X,Z)\Big(U_{P}(X,Z)-\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[W_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z\big]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[W_{P}(X,Z)U_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z\big]\Big),

\displaystyle W_{P}(X,Z)=C_{P}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\varepsilon^{2}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\big]^{-1},\qquad U_{P}(X,Z)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z\big],

for any constant
C_{P}
.
This gives the function
\phi_{P}
corresponding to the efficient influence function of the partially parametric model. Precisely the efficient influence function takes this form (
10
) with specifically
C_{P}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\mathrm{Cov}_{P}\big(\nabla_{\theta}\mu(X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z)),\,\phi_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z\big)\big]
in the above.
∎

A.3
Reparameterisations of the nuisance functions in the partially parametric model

In the partially parametric model defined through the conditional moment specification

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]=\mu(X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z)),

to estimate the nuisance function
f_{P}
it may help in some settings to reparameterise the above model in terms of nuisance functions that are easier to estimate with ‘off-the-shelf’ regression packages (e.g. nuisance functions expressible as a single conditional expectation). For example the partially linear model

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]=X\theta_{P}+f_{P}(Z),

can be reparametrised as

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,Z]+\big(X-\mathbb{E}_{P}[X\,|\,Z]\big)\theta_{P},

in terms of the two nuisance functions
\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,Z],\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[X\,|\,Z]\big)
. Similarly, the generalised partially linear model

g\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]\big)=X\theta_{P}+f_{P}(Z),

can be reparametrised as

g\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]\big)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[g\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]\big)\,|\,Z\big]+\big(X-\mathbb{E}_{P}[X\,|\,Z]\big)\theta_{P},

in terms of the two nuisance functions
\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[g(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z])\,|\,Z\big],\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[X\,|\,Z]\big)
. Under such reparametrisations it is then natural in our robustness framework to take
M_{1}(X)=X
as to estimate
f_{P}
with this approach we would in any case hope that the regression function
\mathbb{E}_{P}(X\,|\,Z=z)
can be estimated well.

A.4
Connection to the generalised method of moments

Here we describe an alternative interpretation of the robust estimating equations (
14
) we consider in terms of an infinite dimensional extension of the generalised method of moments
(
Hansen,, 1982
)
.

To this end, we introduce the following notation. We take
S=(Y,X,Z)
following law
P
, and take
T:=(X,Z)
,
\xi_{j}(T):=M_{j}(X)-m_{P,j}(Z)
and
\varepsilon(S;\theta):=\big({\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\mu(T;\theta,\gamma)|_{\gamma=f_{P}(Z)}}\big)^{-1}\allowbreak({Y-\mu(T;\theta,f_{P}(Z))})
, notationally dropping the dependence in
P
on both terms
\xi_{j}
and
\varepsilon
.

The population version of the method of moments solves the minimisation problem

\underset{\theta\in\Theta}{\text{minimise}}\;\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbf{G}(S;\theta)]^{\top}\,\mathbf{M}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbf{G}(S;\theta)],

(29)

for some
L
dimensional vector of functions
\mathbf{G}:\mathcal{S}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{L}
and fixed, symmetric, positive semidefinite matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}
. Now consider functions
\mathbf{G}
of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}(S;\theta)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j}^{(1)}(Z)\xi_{j}(T)\varepsilon(S;\theta),\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j}^{(L)}(Z)\xi_{j}(T)\varepsilon(S;\theta)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\big(w_{j}^{(1)}(Z),\ldots,w_{j}^{(L)}(Z)\big)\xi_{j}(T)\varepsilon(S;\theta).

The minimisers of (
29
) therefore coincide with solutions to the equation

\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{L}\mathbf{M}_{ll^{\prime}}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j}^{(l)}(Z)\xi_{j}(T)\varepsilon(S;\theta)\bigg]\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{J}w_{j^{\prime}}^{(l^{\prime})}(Z)\xi_{j^{\prime}}(T)\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\bigg]=0,

which can be reposed as

\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j}(Z)\xi_{j}(T)\varepsilon(S;\theta)\bigg]=0,

(30)

where

w_{j}(Z):=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{L}\mathbf{M}_{ll^{\prime}}w_{j}^{(l)}(Z)\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{J}w_{j^{\prime}}^{(l^{\prime})}(Z)\xi_{j^{\prime}}(T)\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\bigg]}_{\text{constant}}.

Note in particular that for the generalised partially linear model

\displaystyle\varepsilon(S;\theta)

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}\big(g^{-1}(X\theta+f_{P}(Z))\big)\big(Y-g^{-1}(X\theta+f_{P}(Z))\big),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{\prime\prime}\big(g^{-1}(X\theta+f_{P}(Z))\big)}{g^{\prime}\big(g^{-1}(X\theta+f_{P}(Z))\big)}X\big(Y-g^{-1}(X\theta+f_{P}(Z))\big)-X,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\,|\,T\big]

\displaystyle=-X,

the latter of which does not depend on
\theta
. The ‘infinite dimensional generalised method of moments’ estimating equations (
30
) therefore takes the form of the estimating equations (
14
) we consider.

A.5
Additional models

As noted in Section
2
the hardness result of Theorem
2
, as well as the asymptotic results of Theorems
3
and
5
, extend to a broader class of models than the partially parametric model. We outline a broader class of models here.

Consider the random variables
(Y,X,Z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{Y}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{Z}}
for some
d_{Y},d_{X},d_{Z}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
all open non-empty sets. Again, we define the tuple
S:=(Y,X,Z)\in\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
. We consider semiparametric models defined through the moment condition

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\varepsilon_{\text{res}}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\,|\,X,Z]=0,

(31)

for some ‘pseudo-residual’
\varepsilon_{\text{res}}:\mathcal{S}\times\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{f}}
and some measurable function
f_{P}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{p_{f}}
, with
p_{f}\in\mathbb{N}
, that also satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\nabla_{\gamma}\varepsilon_{\text{res}}(S;\theta_{P},\gamma)|_{\gamma=f_{P}(Z)}\,|\,X,Z\big]=k(X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))c(Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z)),

(32)

for some
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{f}}\to\mathbb{R}
and
c:\mathcal{Z}\times\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{f}}\to\mathbb{R}^{p_{f}}
. Then the class of functions

\psi(S;\theta_{P},f_{P},\phi_{P})=\phi_{P}(X,Z)\big(k(X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))\big)^{-1}\varepsilon_{\text{res}}(S;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z)).

(33)

for
\phi_{P}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi_{P}(X,Z)\,|\,Z]=0
, are Neyman orthogonal with respect to
(f_{P},\phi_{P})
(Definition
1
), with the robust functions corresponding to
\phi_{P}
taking the form (
13
). Alternatively worded, (
33
) lies in the orthogonal complement of the nuisance tangent space of the model (
31
), and thus is up to a multiplicative constant an influence function of the semiparametric model (
31
).

This broader model also notably includes that of instrumental variable regression, which we now discuss.

A.5.1
Instrumental variable regression

Consider the partially linear instrumental variable model, where given a response
\tilde{Y}\in\mathbb{R}
, covariate / treatment of interest
\tilde{X}\in\mathbb{R}
, set of instrumental variables
\tilde{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\tilde{V}}}
and covariates
\tilde{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\tilde{Z}}}
satisfying

\tilde{Y}=\tilde{X}\theta_{P}+f_{P}(\tilde{Z})+\tilde{\varepsilon},

where
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{\varepsilon}\,|\,\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}]=0
,
f_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\tilde{Z}}}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is the parameter of interest, and
d_{\tilde{V}},d_{\tilde{Z}}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then take
Y=(\tilde{Y},\tilde{X})
,
X=\tilde{V}
,
Z=\tilde{Z}
,
S=(Y,X,Z)=(\tilde{Y},\tilde{X},\tilde{V},\tilde{Z})
, and

\varepsilon_{\text{res}}^{(\text{IV})}(S;\theta,f(Z))=\tilde{Y}-\tilde{X}\theta-f(\tilde{Z}).

Then (
31
) holds, and (
32
) holds with
p_{f}=1
and

k(X,Z;\theta,f(Z))=1,\qquad c(Z;\theta,f(Z))=-1.

The condition
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{\varepsilon}\,|\,\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}]=0
, or equivalently

\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(\tilde{Y}-\theta_{P}\tilde{X})-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{Y}-\theta_{P}\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z}]\,|\,\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}\big]=0,

(34)

is often the primary imposition defining the instrumental variable model; see for example
Chernozhukov et al., (2018)
;
Chen et al., (2021)
;
Liu et al., (2021)
, among others. A sufficient condition for (
34
) is
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{X})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\tilde{V}\,|\,\tilde{Z}
; often this stronger independence is assumed in practice when selecting and justifying valid instruments, and indeed in all empirical examples considered in
Chen et al., (2021)
;
Liu et al., (2021)
the instruments used satisfy this stronger independence. The semiparametric efficient estimator under only the weaker identifiability condition (
34
) has (scaled) efficient influence function

\displaystyle\hskip-120.00018pt\psi_{\text{IV.eff}}(S;\theta_{P},\eta_{P}):=\frac{1}{\varsigma_{P}(\tilde{V},\tilde{Z})}\bigg(\iota_{P}(\tilde{V},\tilde{Z})-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\varsigma_{P}^{-1}(\tilde{V},\tilde{Z})\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z}\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\varsigma_{P}^{-1}(\tilde{V},\tilde{Z})\,|\,\tilde{Z}\big]}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 220.00034pt\cdot\Big(\tilde{Y}-\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\tilde{Y}\,|\,\tilde{Z}\big]-\theta(\tilde{X}-\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z}\big])\Big),

\displaystyle\varsigma_{P}(\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\big(\tilde{Y}-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{Y}\,|\,\tilde{Z}]-\theta_{P}(\tilde{X}-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z}])\big)^{2}\,\big|\,\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}\Big],\quad\iota_{P}(\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}):=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}\big],

where
\eta_{P}
collects the relevant conditional expectation functions given above. Under the stronger independence model
(\tilde{Y}-\theta_{P}\tilde{X})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\tilde{V}\,|\,\tilde{Z}
the (scaled) efficient influence function
\psi_{\text{IV.eff}}
collapses to

\psi_{\text{IV.eff}}(S;\theta_{P},\eta_{P})=w_{P}(\tilde{Z})\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{V},\tilde{Z}]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z}]\big)\big(\tilde{Y}-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{Y}\,|\,\tilde{Z}]-\theta_{P}(\tilde{X}-\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z}])\big),

where
w_{P}(\tilde{Z}):=\mathrm{Var}_{P}(\tilde{Y}-\theta_{P}\tilde{X}\,|\,\tilde{Z})
. Thus, under the stronger independence model, the efficient instrumental variable estimator lies within the robust class of estimators we consider. Thus, the classically semiparametric efficient instrumental variable estimator coincides with the robust semiparaemtric efficient (ROSE) estimator in instrumental variable regression specifically under the stronger independence model
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{X})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\tilde{V}\,|\,\tilde{Z}
.

Appendix B
Influence function calculation in the partially parametric model

Here we formally define the partially parametric model (
8
), and construct the class of influence functions of this model.

Assumption A4

(The partially parametric model)
.

The semiparametric model for the tuple
S=(Y,X,Z)
with distribution
P
satisfies
\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]=\mu(X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))

for some
\mu:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\Theta\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and is such that:

(i)

There exists a unique
\theta_{P}\in\mathrm{int}\,\Theta
and
f_{P}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]=\mu(X,Z;\theta_{P},f_{P}(Z))
.

(ii)

For all
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
the map
\gamma\mapsto\mu(x,z;\theta,\gamma)
on the domain
\mathbb{R}
is differentiable, with
\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\big|\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\mu(x,z;\theta_{0},\gamma)|_{\gamma=f_{0}(z)}\big|>0
and for some positive constant
\omega>0
,
\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\sup_{|\eta|\leq\omega}\big|\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}(x,z;\theta_{0},\gamma)_{\gamma=f_{0}(z)+\eta}\big|<\infty
.

(iii)

The conditional variance function
\sigma^{2}(x,z):=\mathrm{Var}_{P}(Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)
is bounded from above and below by finite positive constants i.e.
\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\sigma^{2}(x,z)>0
and
\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\sigma^{2}(x,z)<\infty
.

(iv)

The random variables
Y
and
(X,Z)
admit a joint density with respect to the product measure
\nu\otimes\lambda
where
\nu
and
\lambda
are
\sigma
-finite measures on
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
respectively.

Note that depending on the semiparametric model Assumption
A4
(i) may restrict the class of semiparametric models beyond that of merely the condition (
8
). For example, in the semiparametric generalised partially linear model, given a law
P
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathrm{Var}_{P}(X\,|\,Z)]>0
the pair
(\theta_{P},f_{P})
are uniquely defined by
\theta_{P}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathrm{Cov}_{P}(g(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]),X\,|\,Z)]}{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathrm{Var}_{P}(X\,|\,Z)]}
and
f_{P}(Z)=\mathbb{E}_{P}[g(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z])\,|\,Z]-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathrm{Cov}_{P}(g(\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,X,Z]),X\,|\,Z)]}{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathrm{Var}_{P}(X\,|\,Z)]}\mathbb{E}_{P}[X\,|\,Z]
. Also for the semiparametric generalised partially linear model Assumption
A4
(ii) is satisfied if the link function
g
is strictly increasing and differentiable.

For the remainder of this section we take
P_{0}
to be the true distribution (as opposed to
P
in the main text), with the corresponding implied parameters/ functions given by
(\theta_{0},f_{0}):=(\theta_{P_{0}},f_{P_{0}})
. In all of this section, all expectations, (co)variances and probabilities should be understood to be under the true distribution
P_{0}
.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Proof of Theorem
1
.

By Assumption
A4
,
Y
and
(X,Z)
have a joint density with respect to the product measure
\nu\otimes\lambda
, where
\nu
and
\lambda
are
\sigma
-finite. Let
p_{0}(x,z)
be the density for
(X,Z)
with respect to
\lambda
, and let
p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)
be the conditional density for
Y\,|\,(X,Z)
with respect to
\nu
, and let
p_{0}(y,x,z):=p_{0}(x,z)p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)
, with the property that

\displaystyle\int y\,p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)=\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)).

Note that here and for the remainder of this section we suppress the dependence of
\mu
above on
\theta_{0}
, which is fixed throughout. Also let
Q_{0}
be the marginal distribution of
(X,Z)
.
We construct the collection of regular parametric submodels given by

\begin{split}p_{t}(y,x,z)&:=p_{0}(x,z)p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z),\\
p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)&:=p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)\bigg(1+\big(\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)\\
&\;\;\cdot\frac{h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]}{\mathrm{Cov}(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)}\bigg)\\
h(u)&:=u\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u|\leq C\}}+C\,\mathrm{sgn}(u)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u|>C\}}\\
f_{t}(z)&:=f_{0}(z)+tg(z),\end{split}

(35)

where
C
is as defined in Lemma
7
, and where
g
satisfies
m\leq\inf_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}g(z)\leq\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}g(z)\leq M
for positive finite constants
M>m>0
. By Lemma
7
there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that for all
t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]
,
p_{t}
is a density in the full semiparametric model (
8
), with

\int y\,p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)=\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z)).

(36)

We now proceed to compute the tangent set of the semiparametric model corresponding to the collection of submodels of the form
\{p_{t}:t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]\}
as the function
g
varies among all bounded functions. Since the parameter
\theta_{0}
is fixed, this is a subset of the (full) nuisance tangent space consisting of the scores of all possible submodels with
\theta_{0}
fixed. Recall
(
van der Vaart,, 1998
, §25.4)
that each influence function is orthogonal to the nuisance tangent space. The tangent set we construct will in fact be the full nuisance tangent set, though this is inconsequential for our purposes: our goal is only to verify that any element of the orthogonal complement of the full nuisance tangent set, and hence any influence function, is of the form given in (
10
).

Now suppose
\varphi\in L_{2}(P_{0})
is a score function corresponding to our path, that is

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\varphi(y,x,z)\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\bigg)^{2}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)=0.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, this implies that for all sequences of functions
H_{t}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\int H_{t}(y,x,z)^{2}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)<\infty,

we have

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int H_{t}(y,x,z)\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\varphi(y,x,z)\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\bigg)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)=0.

Now, using the shorthand
\mu^{\prime}(x,z;\gamma)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\mu(x,z;\gamma)
, take

H_{t}(y,x,z)=\frac{\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)F(x,z)}{\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))g(z)}\big(\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}+\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\big),

(37)

where
F\in L_{2}(Q_{0})
. We then have

\displaystyle\qquad\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}H_{t}^{2}(y,x,z)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq\int\frac{F^{2}(x,z)}{\big(\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}\big(g(z)\big)^{2}}\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\big(\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}+\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\big)^{2}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a^{2}m^{2}}\int F^{2}(x,z)\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}\big(\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}+\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\big)^{2}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{a^{2}m^{2}}\int F^{2}(x,z)\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}\big(p_{t}(y,x,z)+p_{0}(y,x,z)\big)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{a^{2}m^{2}}\int F^{2}(x,z)p_{0}(x,z)\bigg\{\underbrace{\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)}_{\text{$<\infty$ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:valid-density})}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\int\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)}_{<\infty}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}}_{<\infty\text{ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:valid-density})}}\bigg\}d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle<\infty,

where we make use of
\inf_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}|g(z)|\geq m>0
and
\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}|\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))|\geq a
for some constant
a>0
(which exists by Assumption
A4
(ii)). For
H_{t}
as in (
37
) we have that

\frac{1}{2}\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int H_{t}(y,x,z)\varphi(y,x,z)\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)\\
=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\big(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))\big)\varphi(Y,X,Z)F(X,Z)}{\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))g(Z)}\bigg],

where in the above we apply the dominated convergence theorem, which holds because

\displaystyle\bigg(\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big|H_{t}(y,x,z)\varphi(y,x,z)\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\big|d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int\varphi^{2}(y,x,z)p_{0}(y,x,z)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)\Big)\Big(\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}H_{t}^{2}(y,x,z)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)\Big)

\displaystyle<\infty.

Next, define

R_{t}(y,x,z):=\\
\frac{\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)\big(h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]\big)}{\mathrm{Cov}(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)}.

Then by construction

\int p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)R_{t}(y,x,z)d\nu(y)=1,

for any
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
and any
t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]
.
Thus

\displaystyle\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int H_{t}(y,x,z)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}}{t}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int\frac{\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)F(x,z)}{\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))g(z)}\cdot\frac{p_{t}(y,x,z)-p_{0}(y,x,z)}{t}d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int p_{0}(y,x,z)F(x,z)\cdot\frac{\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))}{t\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))g(z)}R_{t}(y,x,z)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\int p_{0}(y,x,z)F(x,z)\cdot\frac{\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))}{t\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))g(z)}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023pt\cdot\bigg\{\underbrace{\int p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)R_{t}(y,x,z)d\nu(y)}_{=1}\bigg\}d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=\int p_{0}(x,z)F(x,z)\cdot\underbrace{\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\bigg\{\frac{\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))}{t\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))g(z)}\bigg\}}_{=1}d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=\int p_{0}(x,z)F(x,z)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[F(X,Z)],

where again we use the dominated convergence theorem as well as Fubini’s theorem; we verify the conditions for each of these now. First, with the positive finite constants
(C,c,\delta_{1})
defined in Lemma
7
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\bigg|H_{t}(y,x,z)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}}{t}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\bigg|\frac{\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)F(x,z)}{\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))tg(z)}\big(p_{t}(y,x,z)-p_{0}(x,y,z)\big)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\bigg|\frac{\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))}{\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z))tg(z)}\bigg|}_{<\infty\text{ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:valid-density})}}\cdot\big|y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big|\cdot|F(x,z)|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\underbrace{\bigg|\frac{h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]}{\mathrm{Cov}(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)}\bigg|}_{\leq 2Cc^{-2}\delta_{1}^{-1}\text{ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:valid-density})}}\,p_{0}(y,x,z)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2}Cc^{-2}\delta_{1}^{-1}\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big|y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big|\cdot|F(x,z)|\,p_{0}(y,x,z).

Then, by Jensen’s inequality and the above inequality,

\displaystyle\quad\bigg(\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\bigg|H_{t}(y,x,z)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{p_{t}(y,x,z)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}}{t}\bigg|d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{4}C^{2}c^{-4}\delta_{1}^{-2}\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\Big(\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}F^{2}(x,z)p_{0}(y,x,z)\Big)d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle=2^{4}C^{2}c^{-4}\delta_{1}^{-2}\int\bigg\{\underbrace{\int\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)}_{<\infty}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}}_{<\infty\text{ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:valid-density})}}\bigg\}\,F^{2}(x,z)p_{0}(x,z)d\lambda(x,z)

\displaystyle<\infty.

Thus we can apply the dominated convergence theorem in the above. To verify the conditions for Fubini’s theorem, it follows immediately from the above that for any
t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]

\int\big|H_{t}(y,x,z)\varphi(y,x,z)\sqrt{p_{0}(y,x,z)}\big|d\nu(y)d\lambda(x,z)<\infty.

Therefore the score function
\varphi
must satisfy the condition that for all
F\in L_{2}(Q_{0})
,

\mathbb{E}\big[F(X,Z)\big]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\big(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))\big)\varphi(Y,X,Z)F(X,Z)}{\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))g(Z)}\bigg],

and so

g(Z)\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))=\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))\big)\varphi(Y,X,Z)\,|\,X,Z\big].

Let
\Lambda_{0}
be the closure of the linear span of the nuisance tangent set of the semiparametric model corresponding to the submodels
\{p_{t}:t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]\}
as
g
ranges over all bounded functions. Then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(g)}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{\varphi\in L_{2}(P_{0})\,:\,\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Y,X,Z)]=0,\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\varphi(Y,X,Z)}{\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))}\,\Big|\,X,Z\bigg]=g(Z)\bigg\},\,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\text{span}\cup_{g}\Lambda_{0}^{(g)}}=\bigg\{\varphi\in L_{2}(P_{0})\,:\,\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Y,X,Z)]=0,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\varphi(Y,X,Z)}{\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))}\,\Big|\,X,Z\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\varphi(Y,X,Z)}{\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))}\,\Big|\,Z\bigg]\bigg\},

with orthogonal complement

\Lambda_{0}^{\perp}=\bigg\{\psi\in L_{2}(P_{0})\;:\;\psi(Y,X,Z)=\frac{\big(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))\big)\phi(X,Z)}{\mu^{\prime}(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))},\,\\
\phi\in L_{2}(Q_{0}),\,\mathbb{E}[\phi(X,Z)\,|\,Z]=0\bigg\}.

As each influence function is orthogonal to the nuisance tangent space
(
van der Vaart,, 1998
, §25.4)
, each nuisance function lies in
\Lambda_{0}^{\perp}
.

∎

Lemma 7
.

Consider the setup of Theorem
1
. There exist finite constants
C,\epsilon>0
such that the function
p_{t}(y,x,z)
given by (
35
) (defined in terms of
C
) is a valid density contained within the semiparametric model (
8
) (i.e. Assumption
A4
) for all
t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]
. Moreover, for such a path

\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)<\infty.

Proof.

By Assumption
A4
(iii) there exist
c_{1},C_{1}>0
such that
C_{1}\geq\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)\geq\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)\geq c_{1}
. Then there exists constants
C>c>0
and
\delta_{1}>0
such that
\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{P}(c\leq|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|\leq C\,|\,X=x,Z=z)\geq\delta_{1}
.

We first show that
p_{t}(y|x,z)
is non-negative for sufficiently small
t
. Recalling the definition of the function
h
(
35
), we have

\displaystyle\quad\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\big(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}\big[(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big]

\displaystyle=\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|<c\}}\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\big[(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{c\leq|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|\leq C\}}\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big]

\displaystyle\quad+C\,\mathbb{E}\big[|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|>C\}}\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big]\big)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}\big[(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{c\leq|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|\leq C\}}\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big]

\displaystyle\geq c^{2}\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{P}\big(c\leq|Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))|\leq C\,|\,X=x,Z=z\big)

\displaystyle\geq c^{2}\delta_{1},

and also

\sup_{(y,x,z)\in\mathcal{S}}\big|h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]\big|\leq 2C,

which together gives

\sup_{(y,x,z)\in\mathcal{S}}\bigg|\frac{h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]}{\mathrm{Cov}(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)}\bigg|\leq 2Cc^{-2}\delta_{1}^{-1}.

We now proceed to bound the quantity
\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}|\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))|
. By the mean value theorem, for each
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
there exists some
\tau(x,z)\in[-|t|,|t|]
such that

\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))=\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{\tau(x,z)}(z))tg(z).

Thus for
t\neq 0
with
|t|\leq M^{-1}\omega

\displaystyle\quad\;\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\big|\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\bigg|\frac{\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))}{\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{\tau(x,z)}(z))tg(z)}\bigg|\cdot\big|\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{\tau(x,z)}(z))\big|\cdot\big|tg(z)\big|

\displaystyle\leq M|t|\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\big|\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z)+\tau(x,z)g(z))\big|

\displaystyle\leq M|t|\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\sup_{|\eta|\leq\omega}\big|\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z)+\eta)\big|

\displaystyle\leq ML|t|,

where
L:=\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\sup_{|\eta|\leq\omega}|\mu^{\prime}(x,z;f_{0}(z)+\eta)|<\infty
; note that
|\tau(x,z)g(z)|\leq M|t|\leq\omega
. Thus for all
|t|\leq\delta_{2}:=\big(M^{-1}\omega\big)\wedge\big(2^{-1}C^{-1}c^{2}\delta_{1}M^{-1}L^{-1}\big)
,

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{(y,x,z)\in\mathcal{S}}\bigg|\big(\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\frac{\big(h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]\big)}{\mathrm{Cov}(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2Cc^{-2}\delta_{1}^{-1}\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\big|\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2Cc^{-2}\delta_{1}^{-1}ML|t|\leq 1.

Thus taking any
0<\epsilon<\delta_{2}
, for
t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]
the function
p_{t}(y,x,z)
is non-negative.

It is also straightforward to show that for such paths

\int p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)=1,\qquad\int y\,p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)=\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z)).

Finally, to verify Assumption
A4
(iii) holds for all distributions in this class,

\displaystyle\quad\;\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}\,p_{t}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\int\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))\big)^{2}p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)

\displaystyle\leq 4\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\int\big(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}p_{0}(y\,|\,x,z)d\nu(y)

\displaystyle\qquad+4\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}\sup_{t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]}\big(\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)^{2}

\displaystyle<\infty,

for any
\epsilon\leq\delta_{2}
; note that the first inequality follows from

\sup_{(y,x,z)\in\mathcal{S}}\bigg|\big(\mu(x,z;f_{t}(z))-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z))\big)\\
\cdot\frac{h(y-\mu(x,z;f_{0}(z)))-\mathbb{E}[h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z)))\,|\,X=x,Z=z]}{\mathrm{Cov}(h(Y-\mu(X,Z;f_{0}(Z))),Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)}\bigg|\leq 1.

∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
2
(the ‘slow rates’ theorem)

In this section we prove Theorem
2
; a hardness result for estimating nuisance functions that do not take particular forms, and by proxy for semiparametric efficient estimation. Our proof builds on classical slow rates results for classification
(
Cover,, 1968
;
Devroye,, 1982
;
Györfi et al.,, 1998
)
. Throughout this section we take
\|\cdot\|
to denote the Euclidean norm and
B_{r}(z):=\{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}:\|z^{\prime}-z\|<r\}
to be the open ball in
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with centre
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and radius
r
. Also throughout we notate
m_{j}(z):=m_{P^{*},j}(z)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[M_{j}(X)\,|\,Z=z]
. Throughout this section all densities are to be interpreted as with respect to Lebesgue measure (all such densities introduced will exist by the assumptions of Theorem
2
). We also define the constants
\bar{\gamma}_{W}:=\sup_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}W(x,z)
and
\underline{\gamma}_{W}:=\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}W(x,z)
, both strictly positive and finite by assumption.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

First note that it is sufficient to show that for all
\alpha>0
, there exists a sequence
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{XZ}
with
\mathrm{TV}(P_{n},P^{*})\leq\alpha
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\underset{n\to\infty}{\limsup}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{P_{n}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{a_{n}}\geq 1.

(38)

To see this, suppose the above holds and take any sequence
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive numbers converging to
0
. Suppose
(P_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N},m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{XZ}
is such that for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence
(P_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the above with
\alpha=b_{m}
. Set
N(0)=0
and then for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
iteratively find
N(m)>N(m-1)
such that

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{m,N(m)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{N(m)}(X,Z)-\phi_{P_{m,N(m)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{a_{N(m)}}\geq 1.

Then let
M(n)=\min\{m:N(m)\geq n\}
and consider the sequence
(P_{M(n),n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Note that
M(n)\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
so
\mathrm{TV}(P_{M(n),n},P^{*})\leq b_{M(n)}\to 0
. Also when
n=N(m)
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that the last display above holds with
P_{M(n),n}
in place of
P_{m,N(m)}
. Thus (
38
) holds with
P_{n}:=P_{M(n),n}
as well, as required. In the following therefore we fix
\alpha>0
and aim to find
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{XZ}
with
\mathrm{TV}(P_{n},P^{*})\leq\alpha
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying (
38
).

Next note that without loss of generality we may take
M_{1},\ldots,M_{J}
and
\cdot\mapsto 1
to be linearly independent functions; otherwise we can iteratively omit each of the functions
M_{j}
that can be written as a linear combination of the others (and if
\mathbb{E}_{P}[M_{j}(X)\,|\,Z=z]=m_{j}(z)
for the remaining functions, where
P
is an arbitrary law, and using linearity of expectations, the omitted functions still satisfy
\mathbb{E}_{P}[M_{j}(X)\,|\,Z=z]=m_{j}(z)
for all omitted
M_{j}
). Also as
P^{*}\in\mathcal{P}_{XZ}
, the distribution
P^{*}
admits the conditional and marginal densities
p^{*}(x\,|\,z)
and
p^{*}(z)
respectively. We define
\gamma:=\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}{p^{*}(z)}<\infty
, and
\iota:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\iota(z):=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[W(X,Z)U(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z]}{\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[W(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z]}.

Then by taking Lemma
8
with
\zeta:=\gamma^{-1}\alpha
, we see that there exists a non-empty open ball
B\subset\mathcal{Z}
, constant
\kappa>0
and conditional density
p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)
such that the following holds:

\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)U(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)U(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}=\kappa,

for all
z\in B
and

\int_{B}\int_{\mathcal{X}}|p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)|dxdz\leq\zeta.

Let
{v}:=\int_{B}dz=\mathrm{Vol}\,(B)
and let
z_{0}
be the centre of the ball
B
. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
we construct a class of distributions on
(X,Z)
parametrised by some
(m,c)_{m\in\mathbb{N},c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}
.
In the following, we fix an
m\in\mathbb{N}
and where clear omit the dependence on
m
. We will first define a marginal distribution on
Z
given in terms of some
q_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
that satisfies
\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}=\frac{{v}}{2}
.
Given an arbitrary
q\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
satisfying
\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{k}=\frac{{v}}{2}
we fix a partition
(S_{k},S^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
of
B
, defined as follows. For
k\in[m]
, define
r_{k}>r^{\prime}_{k}>0
to be such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}\,B_{r_{k}}(z_{0})=2\sum_{l=k}^{m}q_{l}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{Vol}\,B_{r^{\prime}_{k}}(z_{0})=q_{k}+2\sum_{l=k+1}^{m}q_{l}

and set
r_{m+1}:=0
.
Note that then
B_{r_{1}}(z_{0})=B
.
Next define for
k\in[m]
the (thick) shells

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{k}:=B_{r_{k}}(z_{0})\backslash B_{r^{\prime}_{k}}(z_{0})\qquad\text{and}\qquad S_{k}:=B_{r^{\prime}_{k}}(z_{0})\backslash B_{r_{k+1}}(z_{0}).

Note then
(S_{k},S^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
forms a partition of
B
with
\mathrm{Vol}\,S_{k}=\mathrm{Vol}\,S^{\prime}_{k}=q_{k}
.
Also define
S:=\cup_{k\in[m]}S_{k}
. Writing
\beta:={v}^{-1}\int_{B}p^{*}(z)dz
and

\delta:=\frac{\alpha}{2\beta{v}}\wedge\frac{1}{2},

we then define the marginal density

p_{0}(z):=p^{*}(z)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Z}\backslash B}(z)+\big((1-\delta)p^{*}(z)+\delta\beta\big)\mathbbm{1}_{B}(z).

(39)

Now fix some
c\in\{0,1\}^{m}
and define the conditional density

\displaystyle p_{0}(x\,|\,z):=p^{*}(x\,|\,z)+(p^{\dagger}-p^{*})(x\,|\,z)H(z),

(40)

where
p^{\dagger}
is as defined in Lemma
8
and

\displaystyle H(z):=\begin{cases}0&\;\text{if $z\in\mathcal{Z}\backslash B$}\\
c_{k}&\;\text{if $z\in S_{k}$}\\
c_{k}&\;\text{if $z\in S_{k}^{\prime}$ and $c_{k}=c_{k-1}$}\\
\Big(1+\exp\Big(\frac{r_{k}+r^{\prime}_{k}-2\|z-z_{0}\|}{(\|z-z_{0}\|-r^{\prime}_{k})(r_{k}-\|z-z_{0}\|)}\cdot(c_{k-1}-c_{k})\Big)\Big)^{-1}&\;\text{if $z\in S_{k}^{\prime}$ and $c_{k}\neq c_{k-1}$}\end{cases},

where additionally we take
c_{0}:=0
.
Together this defines a joint density
p_{0}(x,z):=p_{0}(x\,|\,z)p_{0}(z)
, which we claim satisfies the following properties:

(i)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}|p_{0}(x,z)-p^{*}(x,z)|d(x,z)\leq\alpha
.

(ii)

The functions
z\mapsto p_{0}(x\,|\,z)
for
x\in\mathcal{X}
are equicontinuous.

(iii)

p_{0}(z)
and
p_{0}(x\,|\,z)
are both bounded away from zero and infinity.

(iv)

\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)U(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}=\iota(z)+\kappa c_{k},

for any
k\in[m]
and
z\in S_{k}
, and where
\kappa>0
is as defined in Lemma
8
.

(v)

\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx=m_{j}(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

We verify each of these claims in turn.

To show
(i)
, first note that

\displaystyle p_{0}(x,z)-p^{*}(x,z)

\displaystyle=\{p_{0}(x\,|\,z)p_{0}(z)-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)p^{*}(z)\}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(z)

\displaystyle=[p_{0}(x\,|\,z)\{(1-\delta)p^{*}(z)+\delta\beta\}-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)p^{*}(z)]\mathbbm{1}_{B}(z)

\displaystyle=[(1-\delta)p^{*}(z)\{p_{0}(x\,|\,z)-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)\}-\delta p^{*}(x,z)+\delta\beta p_{0}(x\,|\,z)]\mathbbm{1}_{B}(z).

Thus using Lemma
8
, we have

\displaystyle 2\text{TV}(P_{0},P^{*})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}|p_{0}(x,z)-p^{*}(x,z)|\,d(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta)\int_{B}p^{*}(z)\int_{\mathcal{X}}|p_{0}(x\,|\,z)-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)|\,dx\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\int_{B}p^{*}(z)\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)\,dx}_{=1}\,dz+\delta\beta{v}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta)\gamma\underbrace{\int_{B}\int_{\mathcal{X}}|p_{0}(x\,|\,z)-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)|\,dx\,dz}_{\leq\zeta\text{ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:theta+c})}}+\delta\underbrace{\int_{B}p^{*}(z)\,dz}_{=\beta{v}}+\delta\beta{v}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta)\gamma\zeta+2\beta{v}\delta

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\zeta+2\beta{v}\delta

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha,

with the last inequality following by definition of the constants
\zeta:=\gamma^{-1}\alpha
and
\delta:=\frac{\alpha}{2\beta{v}}\wedge\frac{1}{2}
above, repeated here for convenience.

Claim
(ii)
follows trivially by construction of (
40
), noting that function

r\mapsto\bigg(1+\exp\bigg(\frac{l_{1}+l_{2}-2r}{(r-l_{1})(l_{2}-r)}\bigg)\bigg)^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{(l_{1},l_{2})}(r)+\mathbbm{1}_{[l_{2},\infty)}(r),

for any
0<l_{1}<l_{2}
, is continuous.

Claim
(iii)
also follows by construction, as
p^{*}(x\,|\,z)
and
p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)
are both bounded away from zero and infinity and
H(z)
is non-negative and bounded by 1.

Claim
(iv)
follows as for any
k\in[m]
and
z\in S_{k}
we have
H(z)=c_{k}
and so
p_{0}(x\,|\,z)=p^{*}(x\,|\,z)\mathbbm{1}_{\{c_{k}=0\}}+p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)\mathbbm{1}_{\{c_{k}=1\}}
and thus alongside Lemma
8
(i),

\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)U(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}=\iota(z)+\kappa c_{k}.

To prove
(v)
, note that in the proof of Lemma
8
we show that

\int_{\mathcal{X}}(p^{\dagger}-p^{*})(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx=0,

for any
z\in B
. Noting also that
\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx=m_{j}(z)
, it then follows

\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx+H(z)\int_{\mathcal{X}}(p^{\dagger}-p^{*})(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx=m_{j}(z),

thus proving
(v)
.

We now introduce some additional notation. We will denote by
\nu^{(m,q)}
the marginal distribution with density
p_{0}(z)
as in (
39
) and by
\lambda^{(m,c)}
the conditional distribution with density
p_{0}(x\,|\,z)
as in (
40
). Together
\nu^{(m,q)}
and
\lambda^{(m,c)}
define a collection of joint distributions
\mu^{(m,c,q)}
for
(X,Z)
, parametrised by
(m,c,q)
.
For any such
(m,c,q)
with
\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}=\frac{{v}}{2}
the distribution
\mu^{(m,c,q)}\in\mathcal{P}_{XZ}
, due to
(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(v)
, and moreover
\text{TV}(\mu^{(m,c,q)},P^{*})\leq\alpha
by
(i)
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
we now
explicitly define a sequence
q_{m}=(q_{m,k})_{k\in[m]}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}=\frac{{v}}{2}
as follows. Define the constant
\tau:=8^{-1}\underline{\gamma}_{W}^{2}\kappa^{2}\delta\beta
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is decreasing and satisfies
\tau^{-1}\geq a_{1}
. Note that by Lemma
12
, for any positive non-increasing sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges to zero such that
a_{1}\leq\tau^{-1}
there exists a sequence
(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
p_{k}\geq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}p_{k}=\frac{{v}}{2}
and

\frac{\underline{\gamma}_{W}^{2}\kappa^{2}\delta\beta}{8}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}p_{k}\Big(1-\frac{2p_{k}}{{v}}\Big)^{n}\geq a_{n},

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then define
(q_{m,k})_{m\in\mathbb{N},k\in[m]}
by

q_{m,k}:=\frac{{v}}{2\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{m}p_{k^{\prime}}}p_{k}.

Note then by construction,
\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}=\frac{{v}}{2}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and

\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}{a_{n}}\geq 1,

(41)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

From hereon we consider the sequences
(q_{m,k})_{k\in[m]}
defined above (
41
) as fixed.
Then,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{XZ}:\text{TV}(P,P^{*})\leq\alpha}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{P}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{a_{n}}\\
\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m,c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m,c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}\cdot\frac{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}{a_{n}},

noting that
\text{TV}(\mu^{(m,c,q)},P^{*})\leq\alpha
.
We will apply Lemma
13
with

x_{n,m}=\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m,c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m,c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}},\,y_{n,m}=\frac{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}{a_{n}}.

As (
41
) has been proved, to prove Theorem
2
via Lemma
13
it suffices to show the following:
for any subsequence
(m_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the natural numbers

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{m_{n},k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m_{n},k})^{n}}\geq 1.

(42)

The remainder of the proof verifies the claim (
42
). Fix a subsequence
(m_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the natural numbers. We will show that there exists some
c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{m_{n},k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m_{n},k})^{n}}\geq 1,

(43)

as then (
42
) follows as

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{m_{n},k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m_{n},k})^{n}}\\
\geq\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{m_{n},k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m_{n},k})^{n}}\geq 1.

We now proceed to prove (
43
).

Consider an arbitrary
c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
D_{n}:=\{(X_{1},Z_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Z_{n})\}
denote
n
i.i.d. copies of data generated from the distribution
\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}
, and let
\mu_{n}
be the joint distribution of
D_{n}
. From hereon, we will omit the dependence on
(m_{n},q)
in the laws
\nu^{(m_{n},q)}
,
\lambda^{(m_{n},c)}
and
\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}
, denoting them by
\nu,\lambda^{(c)}
and
\mu^{(c)}
respectively (recall here we are considering
q
and
(m_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as fixed). We also omit the dependence on
c
in the joint law
\mu_{n}
, and omit the dependence on
m_{n}
in
q_{m_{n},k}
, writing
q_{k}
instead where clear. We will use the shorthand
\mathbb{E}^{\star}
for the expectation conditional on
D_{n}
, and similarly
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
and
\mathrm{Var}^{\star}
.
Consider an arbitrary sequence of estimators
(\hat{\phi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where
\hat{\phi}_{n}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is trained on
D_{n}
.
Also recall that

\phi_{\lambda^{(c)}}(x,z)=W(x,z)\bigg(U(x,z)-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda^{(c)}}[W(X,Z)U(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z]}{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda^{(c)}}[W(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z]}\bigg).

Given
(\hat{\phi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
define the sequences of functions
(\tilde{\varphi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
by

\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(x,z):=U(x,z)-\frac{\hat{\phi}_{n}(x,z)}{W(x,z)},\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z):=\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\lambda^{(c)}}\left[\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z\right],

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Note we denote the dependence on
D_{n}
in such functions through the
n
subscript. We also define

\varphi^{(c)}(z):=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda^{(c)}}\left[W(X,Z)U(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda^{(c)}}\left[W(X,Z)\,|\,Z=z\right]}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\mu^{(c)}}\left[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\lambda^{(c)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\mu^{(c)}}\left[W(X,Z)^{2}\big(\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)-\varphi^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\underline{\gamma}_{W}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\mu^{(c)}}\left[\big(\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)-\varphi^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\underline{\gamma}_{W}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\left[\big(\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)-\varphi^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right].

Note the penultimate inequality holds as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\mu^{(c)}}\left[\big(\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)-\varphi^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\left[\big(\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)-\varphi^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\mu^{(c)}}\left[\big(\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]+\underbrace{2\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\left[\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\lambda^{(c)}}\big[\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}(Z)\,|\,Z\big]\big(\varphi_{n}^{(c)}(Z)-\hat{\varphi}^{(c)}(Z)\big)\right]}_{=0}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\big[\mathrm{Var}^{\star}_{\lambda^{(c)}}\big(\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(X,Z)\,|\,Z\big)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle 0.

Further

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\left[\big(\varphi^{(c)}(Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]\geq\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\left[\big(\varphi^{(c)}(Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{S}(Z)\right]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\int_{S}\big(\varphi^{(c)}(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)\big)^{2}\nu(dz)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\int_{S_{k}}\big(\varphi^{(c)}(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)\big)^{2}\nu(dz).

Now define
\bar{c}_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{m_{n}}
by

\bar{c}_{n,k}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\frac{1}{q_{k}}\int_{S_{k}}\big(\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)-\iota(z)\big)\nu(dz)\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad(k\in[m_{n}]).

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\Big[\big(\varphi^{(c)}(Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\int_{S_{k}}\big(\varphi^{(c)}(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)\big)^{2}\nu(dz),

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\int_{S_{k}}\big(\varphi^{(c)}(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)\big)^{2}\nu(dz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\int_{S_{k}}\big(\iota(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}+\kappa c_{k}\big)^{2}\nu(dz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\bigg(\underbrace{\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\int_{S_{k}}\Big(\iota(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}+\frac{1}{2}\Big)^{2}\nu(dz)}_{\geq 0}+\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\kappa^{2}\underbrace{\Big(c_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)^{2}}_{=\frac{1}{4}}\int_{S_{k}}\nu(dz)

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\Big(c_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)\int_{S_{k}}\Big(\iota(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\Big)\nu(dz)}_{\geq 0}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\int_{S_{k}}\big((1-\delta)p^{*}(z)+\delta\beta\big)dz

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}\delta\beta}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\int_{S_{k}}dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{2}\delta\beta}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})},

where in the sixth line we make use of

\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\Big(c_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)\int_{S_{k}}\Big(\iota(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\Big)\nu(dz)\\
=\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\bigg|\int_{S_{k}}\big(\iota(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(z)\big)\nu(dz)-\frac{q_{k}}{2}\bigg|\geq 0.

Therefore

\mathbb{E}^{\star}_{\nu}\Big[\big(\varphi^{(c)}(Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\Big]\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}\delta\beta}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k})}\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}\delta\beta}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbbm{1}_{(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k},\Omega_{n,k})},

for the event
\Omega_{n,k}:=\{Z_{i}\not\in S_{k}\,(\forall i\in[n])\}
, and so

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{n},\nu}\left[\big(\varphi^{(c)}(Z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{(c)}(Z)\big)^{2}\right]\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}\delta\beta}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{n},\nu}\left(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k},\,\Omega_{n,k}\right).

Now for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
define the function
R_{n}:\{0,1\}^{m_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

R_{n}(c):=\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{n},\nu}\left(c_{k}\neq\bar{c}_{n,k},\,\Omega_{n,k}\right).

Note therefore that

R_{n}(c)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{n}}(\Omega_{n,k})=\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\Big(1-\frac{2q_{k}}{{v}}\Big)^{n}.

Let
(C_{k})_{k\in[m]}
be a sequence of i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables independent of
D_{n}
with
\mathbb{P}(C_{1}=1)=\mathbb{P}(C_{1}=0)=\frac{1}{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[R_{n}(C)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(C_{k}\neq\bar{C}_{n,k},\Omega_{n,k}\,|\,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n,k}}\mathbb{P}\left(C_{k}\neq\bar{C}_{n,k}\,|\,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega_{n,k}\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{k}\Big(1-\frac{2q_{k}}{{v}}\Big)^{n},

noting that
C_{k}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\,\bar{C}_{n,k}\,|\,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
on the event
\Omega_{n,k}
for all
k\in[m_{n}]
. Thus

\frac{R_{n}(c)}{\mathbb{E}\left[R_{n}(C)\right]}\leq 2.

We can therefore apply Fatou’s Lemma;

\mathbb{E}\left[\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{R_{n}(C)}{\mathbb{E}\left[R_{n}(C)\right]}\right]\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{R_{n}(C)}{\mathbb{E}\left[R_{n}(C)\right]}\right]=1.

Recalling that
R_{n}
is a non-negative function, there exists some
c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{R_{n}(c)}{\mathbb{E}\left[R_{n}(C)\right]}\geq 1.

Thus for any subsequence
(m_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the natural numbers

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{m_{n},k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m_{n},k})^{n}}\\
\geq\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m_{n},c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m_{n}}q_{m_{n},k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m_{n},k})^{n}}\geq 1.

Alongside (
41
) i.e.

\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}{a_{n}}\geq 1,

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and applying Lemma
13
completes the proof;

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{XZ}:\text{TV}(P,P^{*})\leq\alpha}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{P}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{a_{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{c\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(m,c,q)}}\Big[\big(\hat{\phi}_{n}(X,Z)-\phi_{\mu^{(m,c,q)}}(X,Z)\big)^{2}\Big]}{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}\cdot\frac{\tau\sum_{k=1}^{m}q_{m,k}(1-2{v}^{-1}q_{m,k})^{n}}{a_{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\,

\displaystyle 1.

∎

C.1
Additional lemmas for the proof of Theorem
2

Lemma 8
.

Adopt the setup of Theorem
2
where additionally we assume the functions
M_{1},\ldots,M_{J}
and
\cdot\mapsto 1
are linearly independent. Given any
\zeta>0
there exists a conditional density
p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)
on
X\,|\,Z
, a non-empty open ball
B\subset\mathcal{Z}
, and a constant
\kappa>0
, such that the following hold:

(i)

\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)U(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)U(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}=\kappa,

for all
z\in B
.

(ii)

\int_{\mathcal{X}}M_{j}(x)p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)dx=\int_{\mathcal{X}}M_{j}(x)p^{*}(x\,|\,z)dx
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z},j\in[J]
.

(iii)

0<\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X},z\in\mathcal{Z}}p^{\dagger}(x|z)\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X},z\in\mathcal{Z}}p^{\dagger}(x|z)<\infty
and the functions
\{z\mapsto p^{\dagger}(x|z):x\in\mathcal{X}\}
restricted to the domain
B
are equicontinuous.

(iv)

\int_{B}\int_{\mathcal{X}}|p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)-p^{*}(x\,|\,z)|dxdz\leq\zeta
.

Proof.

First note that if

\phi_{P^{*}}\not\in\bigg\{(x,z)\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j}(z)\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z)\big):w_{j}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}\bigg\},

then there exists some
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

\phi_{P^{*},0}:=\phi_{P^{*}}(\cdot,z_{0})\not\in\bigg\{x\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j}(z_{0})\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big):w_{j}(z_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\bigg\}.

(44)

Let us define the functions
r:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
and
r_{0},W_{0}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle r(x,z):=W(x,z)U(x,z),\quad r_{0}(x):=r(x,z_{0}),\quad\text{and}\quad W_{0}(x):=W(x,z_{0}).

Given the distribution
P^{*}
, let
p^{*}(x\,|\,z)
denote the corresponding conditional density for
X\,|\,Z
and
p^{*}(z)
denote the corresponding marginal density for
Z
.
We also define the following vector spaces of functions on
\mathcal{X}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}:=\bigg\{x\mapsto\beta_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)\,:\,(\beta_{-1},\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{J+2}\bigg\},

(45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{-}:=\bigg\{x\mapsto\beta_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)\,:\,(\beta_{-1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{J+1}\bigg\}

(46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{0}:=\bigg\{x\mapsto\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)\,:\,(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{J+1}\bigg\}

(47)

The proof of the lemma consists of a number of parts that we briefly outline now:

Part 1:

We show that (
44
) implies either: Case 1:
r_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}
; or Case 2:
r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{G}_{-}
and
W_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}_{0}
.

Part 2:

In each case we construct some set of
J+2
points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{J+2}\in\mathcal{X}
such that certain properties hold (loosely speaking these properties encode information about each of the above cases).

Part 3:

We construct a class of densities
p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)
(parametrised by a scalar
\eta\in\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
where
0\in\mathrm{int}\,\Lambda
) that perturbs
p_{0}^{*}(x\,|\,z)=p^{*}(x\,|\,z)
within sufficiently small
\epsilon
-balls around the points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{J+2}
so as to preserve the
J
conditional expectation conditions required, whilst also enforcing that for a sufficiently small ball
B\subset\mathcal{Z}

\inf_{z\in B}\sup_{\eta\in\Lambda}\Bigg(\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}\Bigg)

is bounded away from zero.

Part 4:

Using the result of Part 3, the function

f(z,\eta):=\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx},

is by construction continuous in
z
, and further we show there exists some
\kappa>0
and
\bar{\eta}:B\to\Lambda
with
f(z,\bar{\eta}(z))=\kappa
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Defining
p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)=p^{*}_{\bar{\eta}(z)}(x\,|\,z)
on
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times B
it then follows for all
z\in B
,

\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{\dagger}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}=\kappa.

By virtue of the perturbed density constructed in Part 3, the conditions (iii) and (iv) will be shown to hold for
p^{\dagger}
. We proceed by showing each of the above parts in turn.

Part 1:

We first note that (
44
) implies either (Case 1):
r_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}
or (Case 2)
r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{G}_{-}
and
W_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}_{0}
. This follows by a simple contrapositive argument; we must show that in the two cases: (A)

r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{-}
, or (B)
r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
and
W_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
, that (
44
) is violated. In the former setting if (A) holds then there exist some
\beta^{(A)}_{-1},\beta^{(A)}_{1},\ldots,\beta^{(A)}_{J}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle r_{0}(x)=\beta^{(A)}_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta^{(A)}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big).

Then for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{P^{*},0}(x)

\displaystyle=r_{0}(x)-\frac{W_{0}(x)\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[r_{0}(X)\,|\,Z=z_{0}]}{\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[W_{0}(X)\,|\,Z=z_{0}]}

\displaystyle=\beta^{(A)}_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta^{(A)}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)-\beta^{(A)}_{-1}W_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta^{(A)}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big),

contradicting (
44
).
In the latter setting of (B), if
r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
and
W_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
then there exist some
\beta^{(B)}_{-1},\beta^{(B)}_{0},\beta^{(B)}_{1},\ldots,\beta^{(B)}_{J},\gamma^{(B)}_{0},\ldots,\gamma^{(B)}_{J}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle r_{0}(x)=\beta^{(B)}_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\beta^{(B)}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta^{(B)}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big),

\displaystyle W_{0}(x)=\gamma^{(B)}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\gamma^{(B)}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big).

Then for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{P^{*},0}(x)

\displaystyle=r_{0}(x)-\frac{W_{0}(x)\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[r_{0}(X)\,|\,Z=z_{0}]}{\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[W_{0}(X)\,|\,Z=z_{0}]}

\displaystyle=\beta^{(B)}_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\beta^{(B)}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta^{(B)}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\beta^{(B)}_{-1}W_{0}(x)-\frac{W_{0}(x)}{\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[W_{0}(X)\,|\,Z=z_{0}]}\beta^{(B)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\beta^{(B)}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)-\frac{\beta^{(B)}_{0}}{\gamma^{(B)}_{0}}\big(W_{0}(x)-\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[W_{0}(X)\,|\,Z=z_{0}]\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\alpha_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big),

where

\alpha_{j}:=\beta^{(B)}_{j}-\frac{\beta^{(B)}_{0}\gamma^{(B)}_{j}}{\gamma^{(B)}_{0}}\quad(\forall j\in[J]),

which contradicts (
44
).

Therefore, taking the contrapositive, under (
44
) we have either: (Case 1)
r_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}
or (Case 2)
r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{G}_{-}
and
W_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}_{0}
. At certain points in the remainder of the proof we will consider these two cases separately.

Part 2:

By Lemma
11
there then exist
J+1
distinct points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{J+1}\in\mathcal{X}
such that

\mathbf{M}^{(0)}_{\text{sub}}:=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}(x_{1})&\cdots&M_{1}(x_{J+1})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
M_{J}(x_{1})&\cdots&M_{J}(x_{J+1})\\
1&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)\times(J+1)}

(48)

is invertible.

We proceed by constructing an additional point
x_{J+2}\in\mathcal{X}\backslash\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{J+1}\}
depending on the two cases above.

Case 1:

r_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}
.

As
\mathbf{M}^{(0)}_{\text{sub}}
(
48
) is invertible, there exists a unique
\bar{r}_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
that interpolates
\{(x_{1},r_{0}(x_{1})),\ldots,(x_{J+1},r_{0}(x_{J+1}))\}
i.e.
\bar{r}_{0}(x_{i})=r_{0}(x_{i})
for each
i\in[J+1]
; also define
\bar{\beta}
to be the
\beta
in (
47
) that gives rise to this
\bar{r}_{0}
, and define
\bar{r}^{\perp}_{0}:=r_{0}-\bar{r}_{0}
.

Now define the function

\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(x):=a_{0}\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x)-(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})W_{0}(x)+(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})\sum_{j=1}^{J+1}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{J+1}W_{0}(x_{i})\big\{(\mathbf{M}^{(0)}_{\text{sub}})^{-1}\big\}_{ij}\bigg)M_{j}(x),

(49)

where

c_{0}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{0}(x\,|\,z_{0})\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x)dx.

Then there exists some
x_{J+2}\in\mathcal{X}\backslash\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{J+1}\}
with
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(x_{J+2})\neq 0
; else
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}\equiv 0
and so by (
49
) it follows that
\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}\in\mathcal{G}
and hence
r_{0}=\bar{r}_{0}+\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}\in\mathcal{G}
contradicting Case 1.

Case 2:

r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{G}_{-}
and
W_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}_{0}
.

As
r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{G}_{-}
there exist some
\tilde{\beta}_{-1},\tilde{\beta}_{0},\tilde{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\beta}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\tilde{\beta}_{0}\neq 0
, such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}

r_{0}(x)=\tilde{\beta}_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\tilde{\beta}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\tilde{\beta}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big).

Further, there exists a unique
\bar{W}_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
that interpolates
\{(x_{1},W_{0}(x_{1})),\ldots,(x_{J+1},W_{0}(x_{J+1}))\}
(as
\mathbf{M}^{(0)}_{\text{sub}}
(
48
) is invertible).
Let
\bar{W}^{\perp}_{0}:=W_{0}-\bar{W}_{0}
.
As
W_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}_{0}
it follows that
\bar{W}^{\perp}_{0}\not\equiv 0
i.e. there exists some
x_{J+2}\in\mathcal{X}\backslash\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{J+1}\}
where
\bar{W}^{\perp}_{0}(x_{J+2})\neq 0
.

Part 3:

We will construct a ‘perturbed density function’
p_{\eta}(x\,|\,z)
as follows.
Consider the equation

{\bf M}(\epsilon){\text{{\boldmath$\eta$}}}(\epsilon)={\bf 0},

(50)

where the matrix
\mathbf{M}(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)\times(J+2)}
is given for
\epsilon>0
by

({\bf M}(\epsilon))_{ji}:=\begin{cases}V_{i}(\epsilon)^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x_{i})}k_{i}(x)M_{j}(x)dx&\text{if }j\leq J\\
1&\text{if }j=J+1\end{cases},\quad k_{i}(x):=e^{-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}-\|x-x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x_{i})}(x),

where
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x_{i}):=\{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}:\|x^{\prime}-x_{i}\|<\epsilon\}
and
V_{i}(\epsilon):=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x_{i})}k_{i}(x)dx
, and for
\epsilon=0
,

(\mathbf{M}(0))_{ji}:=\begin{cases}M_{j}(x_{i})&\text{if }j\leq J\\
1&\text{if }j=J+1\end{cases}.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{(J+1)\times(J+1)}
be the matrix consisting of the first
J+1
columns of
\mathbf{M}(\epsilon)
, and let
\mathbf{m}(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{J+1}
be the vector of its
(J+2)
th column. Note that as
\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}(\epsilon)
is continuous, and
\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}(0)=\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}^{(0)}
which is invertible, there exists some
\epsilon_{1}>0
such that for all
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{1}]
the matrix
\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}(\epsilon)
is invertible. Also define
\mathbf{d}:[0,\epsilon_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}^{J+2}
by

\mathbf{d}(\epsilon):=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}(\epsilon)^{-1}\mathbf{m}(\epsilon)\\
1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{J+2}.

Then for each
i\in[J+2]
the function
d_{i}
is continuous and for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
the vector
{\text{{\boldmath$\eta$}}}(\epsilon):=\eta\,\mathbf{d}(\epsilon)
satisfies the matrix equation (
50
).

Define
\epsilon_{3}:=3^{-1}\min_{i,i^{\prime}\in[J+2],i\neq i^{\prime}}\|x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}}\|
, in addition to taking
\epsilon_{2}>0
as defined by Lemma
9
, and
\epsilon_{4}:=\sup\{\epsilon>0:\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{X}\;(\forall i\in[J+2])\}
, and then take
\epsilon^{*}:=\epsilon_{1}\wedge\epsilon_{2}\wedge\epsilon_{3}\wedge\epsilon_{4}
. Then note that
V_{i}(\epsilon^{*})
carries no
i
dependence; we omit this dependence from hereon. Define
\bar{d}:=\|\mathbf{d}(0)\|_{\infty}\vee\|\mathbf{d}(\epsilon^{*})\|_{\infty}<\infty
. For
{\text{{\boldmath$\eta$}}}(\epsilon^{*}):=\eta\,\mathbf{d}(\epsilon^{*})
it then follows that
\|{\text{{\boldmath$\eta$}}}(\epsilon^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq|\eta|\bar{d}
. Now take an open ball
B^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
centered at
z_{0}
.

Now define

p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z):=\begin{cases}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)+\eta\,V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})k_{i}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}(x)&\quad\text{if }z\in B^{\prime}\\
p^{*}(x\,|\,z)&\quad\text{if }z\in\mathcal{Z}\backslash B^{\prime}\end{cases}.

By construction, the equation (
50
) holds for
{\text{{\boldmath$\eta$}}}=\eta\mathbf{d}(\epsilon)
, and thus

\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)dx=1\,,\quad\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)M_{j}(x)dx=m_{j}(z)\quad(j\in[J]).

Further, as the conditional density
p^{*}
is bounded away from zero, there exists a positive constant
g>0
such that
\inf_{(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times B^{\prime}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)\geq g
. Thus for any
|\eta|<\bar{d}^{-1}g
the function
p_{\eta}^{*}
is strictly positive and thus corresponds to a density satisfying condition (ii). From hereon we therefore restrict
\eta\in\Lambda:=(-\bar{d}^{-1}g,\bar{d}^{-1}g)
.

Now let

\displaystyle D(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z)}{\eta}\Delta(x,z)dx=\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\Delta(x,z)dx,

\displaystyle E(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z)}{\eta}W(x,z)dx=\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)W(x,z)dx,

\displaystyle a(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx,

\displaystyle b(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx,

\displaystyle\Delta(x,z)

\displaystyle:=a(z)r(x,z)-b(z)W(x,z).

Note in particular the quantities
a(z),D(z),E(z)
carry no
\eta
dependence. Also note that by Lemma
10
both
a
and
b
are continuous, and thus
\Delta
is equicontinuous and, as
D(z)
and
E(z)
are expressible as integrals of equicontinuous functions over a compact domain, both
D
and
E
are continuous. Then for each
\eta\in\Lambda
and
z\in B^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\quad\;\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z)}{\eta}\big(a(z)r(x,z)-b(z)W(x,z)\big)dx}{a(z)\Big(a(z)+\eta\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z)}{\eta}W(x,z)dx\Big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta D(z)}{a(z)\big(a(z)+\eta E(z)\big)},

and is zero when
z\in\mathcal{Z}\backslash B^{\prime}
.

We now show that

|D(z_{0})|\geq 2^{-1}\omega,\qquad\omega:=\begin{cases}|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(x_{J+2})|&\text{in Case 1}\\
|\tilde{\beta}_{0}\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{J+2})|&\text{in Case 2}\end{cases}.

Case 1:

r_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}
.
Recalling the notation from part 2, case 1,

\displaystyle r_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{0}(x)+\bar{r}^{\perp}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\bar{\beta}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bar{\beta}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)+\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x).

Then by straightforward calculations

b_{0}:=b(z_{0})=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z_{0})r_{0}(x)dx=\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0}.

Thus writing
a_{0}:=a(z_{0})
,

\displaystyle D(z_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z_{0})}{\eta}\big(a_{0}r_{0}(x)-b_{0}W_{0}(x)\big)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z_{0})}{\eta}\big(a_{0}\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x)-(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})W_{0}(x)\big)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\big(a_{0}\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x)-(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})W_{0}(x)\big)dx.

Defining
\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x):=a_{0}\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x)-(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})W_{0}(x)
, it follows that

\displaystyle|D(z_{0})|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon)^{-1}\int_{{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(0)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}\Big[d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)dx-d_{i}(0)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})\Big]\bigg|

\displaystyle\geq\underbrace{\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(0)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})\bigg|}_{=|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(x_{J+2})|=\omega}-\underbrace{\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}\Big[d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)dx-d_{i}(0)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})\Big]\bigg|}_{\leq 2^{-1}\omega\text{ by Lemma~\ref{lem:holder-ctrl}}}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-1}\omega,

where in the penultimate line (and recalling notation from part 2) we make use of

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(0)\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{J+2})\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}-(\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}^{(0)})^{-1}\mathbf{m}(0)\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})\begin{pmatrix}W_{0}(x_{1})\\
\vdots\\
W_{0}(x_{J+1})\end{pmatrix}^{\top}(\mathbf{M}_{\text{sub}}^{(0)})^{-1}\mathbf{m}(0)+\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{J+2})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(x_{J+2}),

recalling the definition (
49
), and noting that for each
i\in[J+1]
by construction
\bar{r}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{i})=0
and so
\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})=-(\bar{\beta}_{0}+c_{0})W_{0}(x_{i})
.

Case 2:

r_{0}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{G}_{-}
and
W_{0}\notin\mathcal{G}_{0}
.

Recall that in this case

r_{0}(x)=\tilde{\beta}_{-1}W_{0}(x)+\tilde{\beta}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\tilde{\beta}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big).

Therefore

b_{0}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z_{0})r_{0}(x)dx=\tilde{\beta}_{-1}\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z_{0})W_{0}(x)dx+\tilde{\beta}_{0}=a_{0}\tilde{\beta}_{-1}+\tilde{\beta}_{0},

and thus

\displaystyle\Delta(x,z_{0})

\displaystyle=a_{0}r_{0}(x)-b_{0}W_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=a_{0}\tilde{\beta}_{0}-\tilde{\beta}_{0}W_{0}(x)+a_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\tilde{\beta}_{j}\big(M_{j}(x)-m_{j}(z_{0})\big)

thus

\displaystyle|D(z_{0})|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z_{0})}{\eta}\Delta(x,z_{0})dx\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z_{0})}{\eta}\tilde{\beta}_{0}W_{0}(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\tilde{\beta}_{0}\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z_{0})}{\eta}\bar{W}_{0}(x)dx}_{=0\text{ as }\bar{W}_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}}+\tilde{\beta}_{0}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\{p_{\eta}^{*}-p^{*}\}(x\,|\,z_{0})}{\eta}\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x)dx\bigg|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\tilde{\beta}_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(0)\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}\tilde{\beta}_{0}\bigg[d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(z)dx-d_{i}(0)\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{i})\bigg]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\geq\Bigg|\tilde{\beta}_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}d_{i}(0)\underbrace{\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{i})}_{=0\text{ for }i\in[J+1]}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad-\underbrace{\Bigg|\tilde{\beta}_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{J+2}\bigg[d_{i}(\epsilon^{*})V(\epsilon^{*})^{-1}\int_{{\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{*}}(x_{i})}}k_{i}(x)\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(z)dx-d_{i}(0)\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{i})\bigg]\Bigg|}_{\leq 2^{-1}\omega\text{ by Lemma~\ref{lem:holder-ctrl}}}

\displaystyle\geq\big|\tilde{\beta}_{0}\bar{W}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{J+2})|-2^{-1}\omega

\displaystyle=2^{-1}\omega,

noting that
d_{J+2}(0)=1
.

Also note that in both cases

a(z)\big(a(z)+\eta E(z)\big)=\bigg(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx\bigg)\in\big[\underline{\gamma}_{W}^{2},\bar{\gamma}_{W}^{2}\big].

Part 4:

Define the function
f:B^{\prime}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle f(z,\eta):=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\eta}^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)r(x,z)dx}{\int_{\mathcal{X}}p^{*}(x\,|\,z)W(x,z)dx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\eta D(z)}{a(z)\big(a(z)+\eta E(z)\big)}.

Then we have shown in part 3 that

\sup_{\eta\in\Lambda}f(z_{0},\eta)\geq 1.01\kappa,\qquad\kappa:=\frac{\omega g}{2.02\bar{d}\bar{\gamma}_{W}^{2}}>0

Also note that
\eta\mapsto f(z_{0},\eta)
is continuous with
f(z_{0},0)=0
, and so by the intermediate value theorem there exists some
\bar{\eta}(z_{0})\in\mathrm{int}\,\Lambda
such that
f(z_{0},\bar{\eta}(z_{0}))=\kappa
.
Recall as above
a
,
D
and
E
are all continuous functions. Therefore, there exists an open ball
B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq B^{\prime}
centered at
z_{0}
on which
\frac{\kappa a^{2}(z)}{\kappa a(z)E(z)-D(z)}\in\Lambda
. Further, define the open ball
B^{\prime\prime\prime}
to be the open ball centered at
z_{0}
of volume
\frac{\zeta}{G\bar{d}}
, and then define
B:=B^{\prime\prime}\cap B^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Then define
\bar{\eta}:B\to\Lambda
by

\bar{\eta}(z):=\frac{\kappa a^{2}(z)}{\kappa a(z)E(z)-D(z)},

which is itself continuous and thus measurable, with

f(z,\bar{\eta}(z))=\kappa,

for all
z\in B
by construction.
Further
