\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[|\tilde{\Delta}(Z)|\,\big|\,\hat{w}_{1}\big]\leq\mathcal{L}_{\textup{I}}+\mathcal{L}_{\textup{II}}+\mathcal{R}_{\textup{II}}.

(61)

The terms
(\mathcal{L}_{\textup{I}},\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{I}})
and
(\mathcal{L}_{\textup{II}},\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{II}})
act as variance and bias terms respectively, and
(\mathcal{R}_{\textup{I}},\mathcal{R}_{\textup{II}})
act as error terms from the estimators of the nuisance functions.
By (a uniform version of) Slutsky’s Theorem (Lemma
24
), it suffices to show that all these terms are
o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
; we show each in turn.

To bound the
\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{I}}
term

\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{I}}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\,\bigg|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]\leq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\,\bigg|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right].

(62)

Then by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\,\bigg|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\Big)^{2}\,\bigg|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}Q_{i}(Z_{i})Q_{i^{\prime}}(Z_{i^{\prime}})\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}(Z)\omega_{i^{\prime}b}(Z)\,|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

By the honesty property
(P1)
the data used to generate the splits of the tree
T_{jb}
and thus
\omega_{jb}
are independent of the data used for evaluation on the tree indexed by
b
, and thus
\omega_{jb}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}(S_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}}
. Consequently, for any
i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}
it then follows that
\big(Q_{i}(Z_{i}),Q_{i^{\prime}}(Z_{i^{\prime}})\big)\allowbreak\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\allowbreak\big(\omega_{ib}(Z),\omega_{i^{\prime}b}(Z)\big)\,|\,Z_{i}
. In conjunction with the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[Q_{i}(Z_{i})\,|\,Z_{i}]=0
and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}}\omega_{ib}(Z)\omega_{i^{\prime}jb}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})Q_{i^{\prime}}(Z_{i^{\prime}})\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}(Z)\omega_{i^{\prime}b}(Z)\,|\,Z,Z_{\text{train}},\hat{\eta}\right]\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Q_{i}(Z_{i})Q_{i^{\prime}}(Z_{i^{\prime}})\,|\,Z_{i},Z_{i^{\prime}},\hat{\eta}\right]\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}^{2}(Z)\,|\,Z,Z_{\text{train}},\hat{\eta}\right]\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Q_{i}^{2}(Z_{i})\,|\,Z_{i},\hat{\eta}\right]\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}^{2}(Z)Q_{i}^{2}(Z_{i})\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\omega_{ib}^{\frac{2(2+\delta)}{\delta}}(Z)\Big]^{\frac{\delta}{2+\delta}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[|Q_{i}(Z_{i})|^{2+\delta}\Big]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\bigg\{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[|Q_{i}(Z_{i})|^{2+\delta}\Big]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\bigg\}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\max_{i\in[n]}\omega_{ib}^{\frac{4+\delta}{\delta}}(Z)\Big]^{\frac{\delta}{2+\delta}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\bigg\{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[|\partial_{\theta}\psi_{1}(S_{i};\theta_{P},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))|^{2+\delta}\Big]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\bigg\}\big(\min_{l_{b}}k(l_{b})\big)^{-\frac{4+\delta}{2+\delta}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=o(1),

where the second inequality is a direct application of Hölder’s inequality, and the third inequality uses that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(z)=1
.
Thus with (
62
) it follows

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{I}}\big]\leq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)Q_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\right]=o(1).

By Markov’s inequality we thus have
\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{I}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

For the term
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{I}}
we consider the two cases
\delta\geq 2
and
\delta\in(0,2)
separately. If
\delta\geq 2
using the same arguments to that used to bound the term
\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{I}}
gives

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)L_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\right]\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\bigg\{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\psi_{1}^{4}(S_{i};\theta_{P},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\}\big(\min_{l_{b}}k(l_{b})\big)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

If instead
\delta\in(0,2)
then we use the von–Bahr–Esseen inequality on the sequence of mean zero terms
\big(\omega_{ijb}(Z)L_{i}(Z_{i})\big)_{i\in[n]}
. Note these terms are mean zero as

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}(Z)L_{i}(Z_{i})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}(Z)\,|\,Z_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[L_{i}(Z_{i})\,|\,Z_{i}\right]\big]=0,

using the honesty property
(P1)
, and so we can apply the von–Bahr–Esseen inequality;

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)L_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)L_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|^{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}\right]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(2-n^{-1}\big)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}^{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}(Z)|L_{i}(Z_{i})|^{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}\right]\bigg)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(2-n^{-1}\big)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\omega_{ib}^{2+\delta}(Z)\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[|L_{i}(Z_{i})|^{2+\delta}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\bigg(\bigg\{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\big|\psi_{1}(S_{i};\theta_{P},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|^{2+\delta}\right]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\bigg\}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\max_{i\in[n]}\omega_{ib}^{\frac{\delta}{2}}(Z)\right]\bigg)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\bigg(\bigg\{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\big|\psi_{1}(S_{i};\theta_{P},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|^{2+\delta}\right]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}\bigg\}\big(\min_{l_{b}}k(l_{b})\big)^{-\frac{\delta}{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}

\displaystyle=o(1),

and thus in either case

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\mathcal{L}_{\textup{I}}\big]\leq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{ib}(Z)L_{i}(Z_{i})\bigg|\right]=o(1),

and so again by Markov’s inequality
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{I}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

We now show that the ‘bias terms’
\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{II}},\mathcal{L}_{\textup{II}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Note that by Assumption
(M7)

\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{II}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(Z)\big|Q_{i}(Z)-Q_{i}(Z_{i})\big|\,\Big|\,\hat{w}_{1}\bigg]\leq L^{2}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(Z)\left\lVert Z-Z_{i}\right\rVert^{\beta}\,\bigg|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]\\
\leq\frac{L^{2}}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\big(\mathrm{diam}\,l_{b}(Z)\big)^{\beta}\,\big|\,\hat{w}_{1}\Big],

and so it suffices to show for a single tree
T_{b}
that
\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(\mathrm{diam}\,l_{b}(Z))^{\beta}\,|\,\hat{w}_{1}\big]=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. This follows by Lemma
18
,
and so
\mathcal{Q}_{\textup{II}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Analogous arguments show that
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{II}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Finally we show that the error terms
\mathcal{R}_{\textup{I}},\mathcal{R}_{\textup{II}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. For the
\mathcal{R}_{\textup{I}}
term

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\textup{I}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\omega_{i}(Z)\,|\,\hat{w}_{1}\big]|R_{\theta,i}|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\omega_{i}(Z)\,|\,\hat{w}_{1}]^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R_{\theta,i}|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\Big\{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\omega_{i}(Z)\,|\,\hat{w}_{1}]\Big\}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R_{\theta,i}|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}\big(n^{\frac{1}{q}-\lambda}\big)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

using Hölder’s inequality (with
q
such that
q>\lambda^{-1}
and
p=q/(q-1)
, and taking any
\lambda\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
), the fact that (by definition)
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(Z)=1
, and the last step following due to

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[n^{\lambda}\big|\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\hat{\theta},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))-\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\theta_{P},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|\,\big|\,\hat{\eta},\hat{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[n^{\lambda}\big|\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\hat{\theta},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))-\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\hat{\theta},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|\,\big|\,\hat{\eta},\hat{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[n^{\lambda}\big|\partial_{\theta}\psi(S;\hat{\theta},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))-\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\theta_{P},\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|\,\big|\,\hat{\eta},\hat{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[n^{\lambda}\big|\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\theta,\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))-\partial_{\theta}\psi(S;\theta,\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|\,\big|\,\hat{\eta}\right]}_{=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{n^{\lambda}|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{P}|}_{=O_{\mathcal{P}}\big(n^{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}\cdot\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\big|\nabla_{\theta}\partial_{\theta}\psi(S_{i};\theta,\eta_{P}(Z_{i}))\big|\right]

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

following analogous arguments to the proof of Lemma
14
, and utilising Assumption
(M3)
, and the asymptotic normality of the unweighted
\theta
estimator (on a single fold) as shown in the proof of Theorem
5
(and thus, by Markov’s inequality, it follows that
|R_{\theta,i}|=o_{\mathcal{P}}\big(n^{-\lambda}\big)
). Similar arguments show that
\mathcal{R}_{\textup{II}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Therefore all the terms on the right hand side of (
60
) and (
61
) are
o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and so applying a uniform version of Slutsky’s Theorem (Lemma
24
) completes the proof;

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\big(\hat{w}_{1}^{\mathrm{(rose)}}(Z)-w_{P,1}^{\mathrm{(rose)}}(Z)\big)^{2}\,\Big|\,\hat{w}_{1}^{\mathrm{(rose)}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

∎

Lemma 18
.

Under the setup of Theorem
5
we have that

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\mathrm{diam}\,l_{b}(Z)\right)^{\gamma}\,\Big|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

for any
\gamma>0
.

Proof.

Note
Meinshausen, (2006)
proves a result to this effect for a fixed distribution
P
, pointwise in
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, and with
\gamma=1
; our proof here follows their method closely.

Given some
z\in\mathcal{Z}
the rectangular region given by the leaf
l_{b}(z)
that contains
z
for the
b
th tree can be represented by
d
intervals
I_{b}(z;q)
(indexed by
q\in[d]
) as

l_{b}(z)=\otimes_{q=1}^{d}I_{b}(z;q).

Define the terms
N_{b}(z):=\left\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}:Z_{i}\in l_{b}(z)\right\}
and
N_{b}(z;q):=\left\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}:Z_{iq}\in I_{b}(z;q)\right\}
, so
k(l_{b}(z))=N_{b}(z^{\prime})
for any
z^{\prime}\in l_{b}(z)
. Further, let
S_{b}(z;q)
to be the number of times the
q
th variable is split when
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is passed through the tree
T_{b}
. Similarly define
S_{b}(z)
to be the number of nodes passed when
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is passed through
T_{b}
, so
S_{b}(z)=\sum_{q=1}^{p}S_{b}(z;q)
. Also define
S_{b}^{\min}:=\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}S_{b}(z)
. Then

n\alpha^{S_{b}^{\min}}=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\big(n\alpha^{S_{b}(z)}\big)\leq\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{b}(z)=\max_{l_{b}}k(l_{b}(z))=o(1),

by Assumptions
(P2)
and
(P3)
, and so also

\alpha^{S_{b}^{\min}}=o(1).

Now, for any
\phi\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\phi^{\underset{q\in[d]}{\min}S_{b}(Z;q)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\phi^{S_{b}(Z;q)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\phi^{S_{b}(Z;q)}\,\big|\,S_{b}(Z)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq d\,\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(1-(1-\phi)\frac{\pi}{p}\right)^{S_{b}(Z)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq d\,\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\big(\alpha^{S_{b}^{\min}}\big)^{\frac{\log\left(1-(1-\phi)\frac{\pi}{p}\right)}{\log\alpha}}\right],

where the second inequality follows as
S_{b}(Z;q)\,|\,Z\overset{d}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{S_{b}(Z)}Y_{i}\,|\,Z
where
Y_{i}\sim\text{Ber}(p_{i})
for
p_{i}\geq\frac{\pi}{d}

and thus
S_{b}(Z;q)\,|\,Z
is stochastically dominated by
\text{Bin}\big(S_{b}(Z),\frac{\pi}{d}\big)\,|\,Z
, and where we also make use of
y\mapsto\phi^{y}
as a decreasing function on
y\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now, we claim that

A_{b}:=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(1-\alpha)^{\gamma\underset{q\in[d]}{\min}S_{b}(Z;q)}\,\Big|\,T_{b}\right]=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(63)

To prove (
63
) take
\phi=(1-\alpha)^{2}
in the above result, so for any
\epsilon>0

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(A_{b}>\epsilon\right)\leq\epsilon^{-1}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[A_{b}\right]\\
\leq d\,\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\alpha^{S_{b}^{\min}}\right)^{\frac{\log\left(1-\left(1-(1-\alpha)^{\gamma}\right)\frac{\pi}{p}\right)}{\log\alpha}}\right]=o(1),

by applying Lemma
22
to the term
\alpha^{S_{b}^{\min}}
, and noting that
1-\big(1-(1-\alpha)^{\gamma}\big)\frac{\pi}{p}<1
.
Thus
A_{b}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Now, for any
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
q\in[d]

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{I_{b}(z;q)}(Z_{iq})\leq(1-\alpha)^{S_{b}(z;q)},

and so

\max_{q\in[d]}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{I_{b}(z;q)}(Z_{iq})\right)^{\gamma}\leq(1-\alpha)^{\gamma\underset{q\in[d]}{\min}S_{b}(z;q)}.

Thus

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\max_{q\in[d]}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{I(Z;q)}(Z_{iq})\right)^{\gamma}\,\bigg|\,T_{b}\right]\leq A_{b}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(64)

Define

F^{(q)}(t):=\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(Z_{iq}\leq t\big),\quad\text{and}\quad F_{n}^{(q)}(t):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{[Z_{iq},\infty)}(t).

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\mathrm{diam}\,l_{b}(Z)\right)^{\gamma}\,\big|\,\hat{w}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\bigg(\max_{q\in[d]}\left|I_{b}(Z;q)\right|\bigg)^{\gamma}\,\Big|\,T_{b}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\max_{q\in[d]}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\big|F_{n}^{(q)}(t)-F^{(q)}(t)\big|^{\gamma}\,\Big|\,T_{b}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\max_{q\in[d]}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{I_{b}(Z;q)}(Z_{iq})\bigg)^{\gamma}\,\Big|\,T_{b}\right]+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\max_{q\in[d]}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\big|F_{n}^{(q)}(t)-F^{(q)}(t)\big|^{\gamma}\,\big|\,T_{b}\Big]}_{=:B_{b}}+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

and so it suffices to show that
B_{b}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Firstly, note that for every
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\bigg(\max_{q\in[d]}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\big|F_{n}^{(q)}(t)-F^{(q)}(t)\big|^{\gamma}>\epsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{q=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}_{P}\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\big|F_{n}^{(q)}(t)-F^{(q)}(t)\big|>\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2d\,\text{exp}\big(-2n\epsilon^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\big)=o(1)

using the Dvoretzky–Kiefer–Wolfowitz inequality (the upper bound of which is independent of
P\in\mathcal{P}
).
In conjunction with Lemma
22
we have

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[B_{b}\right]=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\max_{q\in[d]}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\big|F_{n}^{(q)}(t)-F^{(q)}(t)\big|^{\gamma}\bigg]=o(1)

and so by Markov’s inequality
B_{b}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
, completing the proof.
∎

E.5
ROSE random forest plus (
J>1
)

Algorithm
3
develops a ROSE random forest for estimators with
J=1
. Here we present an extension for the case
J>1
, designed to estimate the optimal
J
weight functions consistently (see Theorem
19
) in a computationally fast manner. This consistency results is based predominantly on the evaluation criterion being implemented and not the splitting rule. The only reliance on the tree splitting in the consistency results are through Assumption
A3
. Specifically, the splitting rule we employ is likely suboptimal in finite finite samples, but regardless our extension provides a computationally lean pilot estimator that still demonstrates valid asymptotic (Theorem
19
) and promising practical (Section
5.3
) performance.

We now motivate the ROSE random forest plus algorithm. Given an estimator
\hat{\eta}
of
\eta_{P}
and a pilot estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}}
of
\theta_{P}
(achieved via e.g. the zero of the unweighted
\psi
function), and data indexed by the index set
\mathcal{I}
, the empirical sandwich loss (
25
) is

\hat{L}_{\mathrm{SL}}(w):=\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}w_{j}(Z_{i})\partial_{\theta}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\Bigg)^{-2}\\
\cdot\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{J}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}w_{j}(Z_{i})w_{j^{\prime}}(Z_{i})\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\psi_{j^{\prime}}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\Bigg).

Suppose for each
j\in[J]
the state space
\mathcal{Z}
for
Z
is partitioned into disjoint rectangular regions
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
i.e.
\mathcal{Z}=\uplus_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}R
for all
j\in[J]
, and suppose
w_{j}
is piece-wise constant given this partition i.e. for all
j\in[J]
, and for
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
,
w_{j}(Z_{i})=w_{j,R}
if
i\in I_{j,R}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}:Z_{i}\in R\}
. Under this weight model the sandwich loss becomes

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{\mathrm{SL}}(w)

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}w_{j,R}\sum_{i\in I_{j,R}}\partial_{\theta}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\Bigg)^{-2}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{J}\sum_{R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j^{\prime}}}w_{j,R}w_{j^{\prime},R^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in I_{j,R}\cap I_{j^{\prime},R^{\prime}}}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\psi_{j^{\prime}}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\big({\bf w}^{\top}{\bf a}\big)^{-2}\big({\bf w}^{\top}{\bf{F}}{\bf w}\big),

where

\displaystyle{\bf w}

\displaystyle:=\text{vec}\big((w_{j,R})_{j\in[J],R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}\big),

(65)

\displaystyle{\bf a}

\displaystyle:=\text{vec}\big((a_{j,R})_{j\in[J],R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}\big),\qquad a_{j,R}:=\sum_{i\in I_{j,R}}\partial_{\theta}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i})),

(66)

\displaystyle{\bf F}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}(F_{11,RR^{\prime}})_{R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2}}&\cdots&(F_{1J,RR^{\prime}})_{R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}\times\mathcal{R}_{J}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
(F_{J1,RR^{\prime}})_{R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{J}\times\mathcal{R}_{1}}&\cdots&(F_{JJ,RR^{\prime}})_{R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{J}^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

(67)

\displaystyle F_{jj^{\prime},RR^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i\in I_{j,R}\cap I_{j^{\prime},R^{\prime}}}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\psi_{j^{\prime}}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i})).

The optimal weights for this partition are

{\bf w}:={\bf F}^{-1}{\bf a},

(68)

up to a constant multiplicative factor, following by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. The minimising variance is thus

\hat{L}_{\mathrm{SL}}({\bf w})=\big({\bf a}^{\top}{\bf F}^{-1}{\bf a}\big)^{-1}
. As such the quantity
{\bf a}^{\top}{\bf F}^{-1}{\bf a}
can be used to motivate a splitting rule for decision trees, albeit a computationally more involved splitting rule. Note in particular that
{\bf F}
takes the form of a
J\times J
block matrix, with diagonal blocks being diagonal matrices. As such, for the special case of
J=1
the minimising weights take the form
{\bf F}^{-1}{\bf a}=(F_{11,RR}^{-1}a_{1R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and similarly the reciprocal of the minimising variance takes a simpler form
{\bf a}^{\top}{\bf F}^{-1}{\bf a}=\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}F_{11,RR}^{-1}a_{1R}^{2}
, so that the goodness of fit metric as in ROSE decision trees for
J=1
can be written as a function of the data only contained within the candidate region to be split; this property drives the computational leanness of ROSE decision trees matching that of classical CART random forests.

For a general
J>1
however such quantities used for splitting are of a greater computational complexity. To trade off some finite sample optimality for favourable computation, we propose a computationally lean alternative to the ROSE random forest (ROSE random forest plus). The (potentially somewhat suboptimal) splits are generated using the ROSE decision tree algorithm (Algorithm
2
) as if only that individual
\psi_{j}
were to be used. Given such splits (obtained from
J
runs of Algorithm
2
) the joint optimal weight functions
(w_{j})_{j\in[J]}
can be estimated using the evaluation criterion (
68
). A forest can then be generated from such trees by a mean aggregation. The full algorithm is given as Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

ROSE random forests (
J>1
finite)

Input:

Data
\left(S_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
associated with the
\psi
function (
16
); estimator
\hat{\eta}
of nuisance functions; pilot estimator
\hat{\theta}
of
\theta_{P}
;
c_{\text{split}}>0
proportion of data chosen for each tree in the forest; ROSE tree hyperparameters.

for

b\in[B]

do

Generate random subsets of the data
\mathcal{I}_{\text{split},b}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}
as disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{I}
with
|\mathcal{I}_{\text{split},b}|,|\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}|\approx 2^{-1}c_{\text{split}}|\mathcal{I}|
(by bootstrapping or sampling without replacement).

for

j\in[J]

do

Grow a ROSE decision tree
T_{jb}
as in Algorithm
2
(using only the
b
th split and evaluation data
\mathcal{I}_{\text{split},b}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\text{eval},b}
), outputting leaves
\mathcal{L}_{jb}
and leaf index sets
(I_{jb,l})_{l\in\mathcal{L}_{jb}}
.

end for

for

j\in[J]
and
l\in\mathcal{L}_{jb}

do

Calculate
a_{jl,b}=\sum_{i\in I_{jl,b}}\partial_{\theta}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))
.

for

j^{\prime}\in[J]
and
l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{j^{\prime}b}

do

Calculate
F_{jj^{\prime},ll^{\prime}}=\sum_{i\in I_{jl,b}\cap I_{j^{\prime}l^{\prime},b}}\psi_{j}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))\psi_{j^{\prime}}(S_{i};\hat{\theta}_{\text{init}},\hat{\eta}(Z_{i}))
.

end for

end for

Construct
{\bf a}_{b}=\text{vec}\big((a_{jl,b})_{j\in[J],l\in\mathcal{L}_{jb}}\big)
.

Construct
{\bf F}_{b}=\begin{pmatrix}(F_{11,ll^{\prime}})_{l,l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{1b}^{2}}&\cdots&(F_{1J,ll^{\prime}})_{l,l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{1b}\times\mathcal{L}_{Jb}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
(F_{J1,ll^{\prime}})_{l,l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{Jb}\times\mathcal{L}_{1b}}&\cdots&(F_{JJ,ll^{\prime}})_{l,l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{Jb}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Calculate
{\bf\hat{w}}_{b}={\bf F}_{b}^{-1}{\bf a}_{b}
, giving weight values
(\hat{w}_{jlb})_{j\in[J],l\in\mathcal{L}_{jb}}
as in (
65
)

end for

for

j\in[J]

do

Calculate

\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}(z)=B^{-1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{L}_{jb}}\mathbbm{1}_{(z\in l)}\hat{w}_{jlb}(z)

end for

Output:

ROSE random forest weight functions
\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
for each
j\in[J]
.

We present consistency of ROSE random forest plus (Algorithm
4
) analogous to that of Theorem
5
for
J=1
ROSE random forests. Note however that whilst Theorem
19
provides theoretical justification on the use of Algorithm
4
through the asymptotic optimality of the evaluation criterion used in Algorithm
4
, the proof only relies on the splitting rule for trees through the properties of Assumption
A3
.

Theorem 19

(Consistency of ROSE random forests plus)
.

Let Assumptions
A1
and
A2
hold for a class of distributions
\mathcal{P}
.
Then, for
J\geq 1
finite, the ROSE random forest plus estimator
\hat{w}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}
(Algorithm
4
, satisfying Assumption
A3
) is uniformly consistent in estimating the optimal weights (
26
): for each
j\in[J]
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\big(\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}(Z)-w_{P,j}^{\mathrm{(rose)}}(Z)\big)^{2}\,\Big|\,\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}\Big]=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

Proof.

Note that as the splitting construction of
\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}
(as in Algorithm
4
) is separable over
j\in[J]
, it suffices (by Lemma
21
) to prove that for a single fixed
j\in[J]

\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[\big(\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}(Z)-w_{P,j}^{\mathrm{(rose)}}(Z)\big)^{2}\,\big|\,\hat{w}_{j}^{\mathrm{(rose+)}}\Big]=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

The proof then follows by analogous arguments to that of Theorem
5
(see Appendix
E.4
).
∎

Appendix F
Further details of numerical experiments in Section
5

We present further details and additional simulation resutls for the numerical experiments of Section
5
.

F.1
Further details for Figure
2

For the experiment of Figure
2
a, we study the following partially linear model. Take
n=20\,000
iid instances of the partially linear model with
Z\sim N_{10}\big({\bf 0},(0.9^{|j-k|})_{(j,k)\in[10]^{2}}\big)
and

\displaystyle p(Z):=\text{expit}(3Z_{1})\vee 0.01,\quad B\,|\,Z\sim\text{Ber}(p(Z)),\quad X\,|\,(Z,B)\sim N\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{5}\text{expit}(Z_{j})\;,\;\frac{B+0.01}{p(Z)}\bigg),

\displaystyle Y\,|\,(X,Z,B)\sim N\bigg(\theta_{0}X+\sum_{j=1}^{5}\text{expit}(Z_{j})\;,\;\frac{B+0.01}{\sqrt{p(Z)}}\bigg).

The results are presented in Figure
2
a.

F.2
Simulation 1: Further details

F.2.1
Hyperparameter tuning details

For both simulations, the nuisance functions
(l_{0},m_{0})
are fitted using random forests with the
ranger
package
(
Wright and Ziegler,, 2017
)
. Hyperparameter tuning is performed for the parameters in the grid
\texttt{sample.fraction}\allowbreak\in\{0.01,\allowbreak 0.05,0.1\allowbreak,0.2\allowbreak,0.5\allowbreak,0.8\allowbreak,1.0\}
and
\texttt{min.node.size}\in\{10\allowbreak,20\allowbreak,30\allowbreak,40\allowbreak,50\allowbreak,100\allowbreak,200\allowbreak,500\allowbreak,1000\allowbreak,2000\}
(note that, except in the case if
\texttt{sample.fraction}=1.0
no tuning selects parameters on the extremes of this grid). All other parameters are taken as default as per the
ranger
package, in particular: 500 trees per forest; the number of variables considered for a split at each node
\texttt{mtry}=\big\lceil\sqrt{d}\big\rceil=4
); and data sampling for each tree performed with replacement. For the hyperparameter tuning cross-validation was performed (with CV criterion of the out-of-bag squared error) with both ROSE random forests and locally efficient CART random forests having optimal maximum depth of two and three in Simulations 1a and 1b respectively.

For Simulation 1a, performing cross-validation selected for both
\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,Z]
and
\mathbb{E}[X\,|\,Z]
estimators
(\texttt{sample.fraction},\allowbreak\texttt{min.node.size})\allowbreak=(0.05,20)
. For the semiparametric efficient estimator, the additional nuisance functions
(\sigma_{0},h_{0})
(as in Section
1.1
) must be estimated, and are estimated via the same cross validation scheme, selecting optimal hyperparameter values of
(\texttt{sample.fraction},\texttt{min.node.size})=(1.0,100)
for the
\sigma_{0}
-learner and
(\texttt{sample.fraction},\texttt{min.node.size})=(1.0,20)
for the
h_{0}
-learner. Note that the optimal
sample.fraction
parameter is much larger for the
h_{0}
-learner than the
m_{0}
-learner; a practical indication of the informal (motivating) notion that the
h_{0}
nuisance function may be a ‘harder’ nuisance function to estimate than the
m_{0}
nuisance function (see e.g. Figure
1
).

For Simulation 1b, cross validation is performed via the same scheme as for Simulation 1a, in this case for the three nuisance functions
\big(\mathbb{E}[X\,|\,Z],\allowbreak\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,X,Z],\allowbreak\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,X,Z])\,|\,Z]\big)
, selecting optimal hyperparameters of
\texttt{min.node.size}=10
for all three nuisance functions, and
\texttt{sample.fraction}=0.05,1.0
and
0.1
for the three nuisance functions respectively. For the semiparametric efficient estimator, the two additional nuisance functions
\sigma_{0}(X,Z):=\mathbb{E}[g^{\prime}(\mu(X,Z))^{2}(Y-\mu(X,Z))^{2}\,|\,X,Z]
and
h_{0}(Z):=\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{0}^{-2}(X,Z)\,|\,Z]^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{0}^{-2}(X,Z)X\,|\,Z]
are estimatied by the same cross-validation scheme, selecting
(\texttt{min.node.size},\allowbreak\texttt{sample.fraction})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(100,\allowbreak 1.0)
and
(20,\allowbreak 0.5)
respectively.

F.3
Simulation 2: Further details

F.3.1
Hyperparameter tuning details

To estimate weights in both cases we use minimum node size 10, and select data for each tree by random selection with replacement (with
sample.fraction
=1). We consider trees of depth 1 to 15 (note that the optimal trees in every simulation are not at the extrema in both cases) with the optimal tree depth selected via cross-validation. For the ROSE random forests we use the sandwich loss cross-validation criterion. For the CART random forests we use the standard prediction error CART cross-validation criterion.

F.3.2
Additional details on simulation results

Figure
7
displays the mean squared error of the
\theta_{0}
estimators (weighted with ROSE and CART random forests) for each tree depth (ranging from 0 to 15). In particular, the suboptimal performance of CART random forests in this simulation are likely a result of two properties: a suboptimal splitting rule; and the CART cross-validation criterion not targetting the mean squared error of the resulting
\theta_{0}
estimator as accurately as the sandwich loss cross-validation criterion. In the example given in Figure
7
any choice of fixed tree depth from 5 to 9 would actually perform better than carrying out cross-validation to find the supposed ‘optimal’ tree depth. This suggests perhaps a wider concern for CART random forests in this setting; it appears performing hyperparameter tuning over a wider range of hyperparameters for the CART random forests can result in poorer performance for the inferential task of interest.

Figure 7:
Mean squared error (as a ratio to the unweighted estimator) of estimators weighted by ROSE and CART random forests with varying tree depths for the simulation of Section
5.2
with
n=6400
(1000 simulations).

F.4
Simulation 3: Further details

F.4.1
Hyperparameter tuning details

As in Simulations 1 and 2 all nuisance functions except for
\mathbb{P}(X\,|\,Z)
are fitted with random forests using the
ranger
package, with hyperparameter tuning for
\texttt{sample.fraction}\in\{0.01,\allowbreak 0.05,\allowbreak 0.1,\allowbreak 0.2,\allowbreak 0.5,\allowbreak 0.8,\allowbreak 1.0\}
and
\texttt{min.node.size}\in\{10,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 40,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 100,\allowbreak 200,\allowbreak 500,\allowbreak 1000,\allowbreak 2000\}
. All other hyperparameters are taken as the
ranger
package default parameters (for example 500 trees per forest). For each sample size
n
, to determine the optimal hyperparameters a grid search is used, with cross validation-criterion for all nuisance function the squared error (or in the case of estimating the nuisance function
h_{0}
the weighted least squared error). The nuisance function
\mathbb{P}(X\,|\,Z)
is fitted using probability random forests via the
grf
package
(
Tibshirani et al.,, 2024
)
with hyperparameter tuning as above. For the two ROSE random forests the maximum depth was taken to be 5 for every sample size; this was the optimal hyperparameter obtained via cross-validation with the sandwich loss criterion.

) are presented in Table

. For a discussion see Section

F.5
Real-world data: Further details

For the real-world data example, all nuisance functions are fit with random forests using the
ranger
package
(
Wright and Ziegler,, 2017
)
and consist of 500 trees, with hyperparameters selected for each random forest using the
tuneRanger
package
(
Probst et al.,, 2018
)
. In the case of the ROSE random forest weight function, each forest consists of 500 trees, each of maximum depth 20 and minimum node size 5. All estimators employ cross-fitting (DML2) with
K=10
folds.

Appendix G
Auxiliary lemmas for uniform convergence results

For the uniform convergence results we prove we require the following lemmas.

Lemma 20
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real-valued random variables. Then

(i)

If
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and
Y_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
then
X_{n}+Y_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

(ii)

If
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and
Y_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
then
X_{n}Y_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

(iii)

If
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and
Y_{n}\leq X_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
then
Y_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

(iv)

If there exists some
\alpha\geq 1
such that
\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left|X_{n}\right|^{\alpha}\big|Y_{n}\right]=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
then
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Lemma 21
.

Let
(X_{n}^{(1)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\ldots,(X_{n}^{(K)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real-valued random variables satisfying
X_{n}^{(k)}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
for all
k\in[K]
, where
K\in\mathbb{N}
is finite. Then
\max_{k\in[K]}X_{n}^{(k)}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Proof.

For any
\epsilon>0

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\Big(\big|\max_{k\in[K]}X_{n}^{(k)}\big|>\epsilon\Big)\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\Big(\max_{k\in[K]}\big|X_{n}^{(k)}\big|>\epsilon\Big)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\Big(\big|X_{n}^{(k)}\big|>\epsilon\Big)=o(1).

∎

Lemma 22

(
Shah and Peters, (2020)
, Lemma 25)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of non-negative real-valued random variables such that
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Suppose
X_{n}\leq C
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
for some
C>0
. Suppose
\alpha\geq 1
. Then

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[X_{n}^{\alpha}\right]=o(1).

Proof.

Shah and Peters, (2020)
proves the following result for
\alpha=1
(the extension to
\alpha\geq 1
given here follows trivially).
For any
\epsilon>0
,

X_{n}^{\alpha}=X_{n}^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{\left|X_{n}\right|>\epsilon\right\}}+X_{n}^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{\left|X_{n}\right|\leq\epsilon\right\}}\leq C^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{\left|X_{n}\right|>\epsilon\right\}}+\epsilon^{\alpha},

and so

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[X_{n}^{\alpha}\right]\leq C^{\alpha}\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}>\epsilon)+\epsilon^{\alpha}=o(1)+\epsilon^{\alpha},

as
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Taking
\epsilon\downarrow 0
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 23

(
Shah and Peters, (2020)
, Lemma 18)
.

Let
(X_{n,i})_{n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n]}
be a triangular array of real-valued random variables satisfying: (i)
X_{n,1},\ldots,X_{n,n}
are independent; (ii)
\mathbb{E}_{P}[X_{n,i}]=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n],P\in\mathcal{P}
; (iii) there exists some
\Delta>0
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[X_{n,i}^{2}\big]\bigg)^{-\big(1+\frac{\Delta}{2}\big)}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[|X_{n,i}|^{2+\Delta}\big]\bigg)=0.

Then
S_{n}:=\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}[X_{n,i}^{2}]\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{n,i}\big)
converges uniformly in distribution to
N(0,1)
i.e.

\lim_{I\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{I}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(S_{I}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|=0.

Lemma 24

(
Shah and Peters, (2020)
, Lemma 20)
.

Let
\left(X_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\left(Y_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real-valued random variables. Suppose

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(X_{n}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|=0.

Then we have:

(a)

\text{If }Y_{I}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)\text{ then }\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(X_{n}+Y_{n}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|=0;

(b)

\text{If }Y_{n}=1+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)\text{ then }\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(X_{n}/Y_{n}\leq t\right)-\Phi(t)\right|=0.

Lemma 25
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real-valued random variables that is uniformly asymptotically Gaussian

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(X_{n}\geq t\big)-\Phi(t)\right|=0.

(69)

Then for any
q\geq 1
,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[|X_{n}|^{q}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim N(0,1)}\big[|Z|^{q}\big]+o(1)=O(1),

where
N(0,1)
denotes a standard normal random variable.

Proof.

Using the ‘layer-cake’ formula

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|X_{n}|^{q}\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(|X_{n}|^{q}>t\big)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(|X_{n}|\geq t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big\{1-\Phi\big(t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)+\Phi\big(-t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)\Big\}dt-\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(X_{n}\leq t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)-\Phi\big(t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)\Big\}dt

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(X_{n}\leq-t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)-\Phi\big(-t^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)\Big\}dt

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[|Z|^{q}\big]-q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\Big\}dt

\displaystyle\qquad+q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq-t)-\Phi(-t)\Big\}dt.

where
Z\sim N(0,1)
. Now, we claim that there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
and all
P\in\mathcal{P}

\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\geq t)\leq 2(1-\Phi(t))\qquad(\forall t\in\mathbb{R}).

We show this claim holds by contradiction now. Suppose the claim does not hold; for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exists some
n_{0}\geq N
, some
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
and some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}>t_{0})>2(1-\Phi(t)).

Then

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|\geq\left|\mathbb{P}_{P_{0}}(X_{n}\leq t_{0})-\Phi(t_{0})\right|\\
\geq\left|\mathbb{P}_{P_{0}}(X_{n}>t_{0})\right|-\left|1-\Phi(t_{0})\right|>|1-\Phi(t_{0})|,

and thus

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|\geq|1-\Phi(t_{0})|,

contradicting (
69
).
Therefore, there exists some
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{n\geq N}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\geq t)-(1-\Phi(t))\right|\leq(1-\Phi(t)).

Given
\epsilon>0
, let
N_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that for all
n\geq N_{\epsilon}
and all
P\in\mathcal{P}

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|\leq\epsilon.

Then for all
n\geq N_{\epsilon}\vee N^{\prime}

\displaystyle\left|q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\Big\}dt\right|

\displaystyle\leq q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|dt

\displaystyle\leq q\int_{0}^{M}t^{q-1}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|dt+q\int_{M}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\left|\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|dt

\displaystyle\leq M^{q}\epsilon+q\int_{M}^{\infty}t^{q-1}(1-\Phi(t))dt

\displaystyle=M^{q}\epsilon+I(M),

where

I(M):=\int_{M}^{\infty}\frac{t^{q}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}dt.

Note that
\lim_{M\to\infty}I(M)=0
. Then taking
M:=\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{q+1}}

\left|q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\Big\}dt\right|\leq\epsilon^{\frac{1}{q+1}}+I(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{q+1}}).

As
\epsilon>0
was taken to be arbitrary, taking
\epsilon\downarrow 0
yields

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\Big\}dt=o(1).

Analogous arguments similarly show

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Big\{\mathbb{P}_{P}(X_{n}\leq-t)+\Phi(-t)\Big\}=o(1),

and thus

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[|X_{n}|^{q}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim N(0,1)}\big[|Z|^{q}\big]+o(1).

The result then follows as
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim N(0,1)}\big[|Z|^{q}\big]=2^{\frac{q-1}{2}}\pi^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\big(\frac{q}{2}+1\big)<\infty
for any finite
q\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 26
.

Let
(\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R})_{P\in\mathcal{P},n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of random real-valued functions and
(\Psi_{P}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R})_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
be a set of deterministic functions such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\big|\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\theta)-\Psi_{P}(\theta)\big|=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

and for every
\epsilon>0

\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\inf_{\underset{|\theta-\theta_{P}|\geq\epsilon}{\theta\in\Theta}}\Psi_{P}(\theta)-\Psi_{P}(\theta_{P})>0.

Then any sequence of estimators
\hat{\theta}_{n}
with
\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\theta_{P})+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
converges uniformly in probability to
\theta_{P}
;

\hat{\theta}_{n}=\theta_{P}+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

Proof.

We extend the proof of Theorem 5.7 of
van der Vaart, (1998)
to the analogous result with uniform convergence in probability. Using the property of the sequence of estimators
(\hat{\theta}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as well as the first of the assumptions of the theorem, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{P}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\Psi_{P}(\theta_{P})

\displaystyle=\big(\Psi_{P}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\big)+\big(\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\theta_{P})-\Psi_{P}(\theta_{P})\big)+\big(\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\theta_{P})\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\big|\hat{\Psi}_{P,n}(\theta)-\Psi_{P}(\theta_{P})\big|+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

By the second part of the assumption, for any
\epsilon>0
there exists some
\nu_{\epsilon}>0
such that
\Psi_{P}(\theta)\geq\Psi_{P}(\theta_{P})+\nu_{\epsilon}
for any
\theta\in\Theta
satisfying
|\theta-\theta_{P}|>\epsilon
.
Hence,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{P}|>\epsilon\big)\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\big(\Psi_{P}(\theta)\geq\Psi_{P}(\theta_{P})+\nu_{\epsilon}\big)=o(1),

thus
\hat{\theta}_{n}=\theta_{P}+o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.
∎
